\left\{\begin{array}[]{ l l}a^{ij}\partial_{ij}u+b^{i}\partial_{i}u+cu=f&\textrm{in $\Omega$},\\
-a^{nj}\partial_{j}u=g&\textrm{along $\partial^{+}\Omega$}.\end{array}\right.

(2.2.1)

To formulate a weak formulation, we consider test functions from the space

W^{k,2}_{0}(\Omega):=\set{\varphi\in W^{k,2}(\Omega^{+})\ }{\ \exists V\subset\subset\Omega\textrm{ open such that }\varphi\equiv 0\textrm{ on }\Omega\backslash V\textrm{ almost everywhere}}.

We stress that
V\subset\Omega
is open in the relative topology, so
\varphi\in W^{k,2}_{0}(\Omega)
need not vanish along
\partial^{+}\Omega
. Putting
\tilde{b}^{i}:=b^{i}-\partial_{j}a^{ij}
, we say that a function
u\in W^{1,2}(\Omega^{+})
is a weak solution of (
2.2.1
) if for all
\varphi\in W^{1,2}_{0}(\Omega)

\int_{\Omega^{+}}-a^{ij}\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}\varphi+\tilde{b}^{i}\partial_{i}u\varphi+cu\varphi=\int_{\Omega^{+}}f\varphi-\int_{\partial^{+}\Omega}g\varphi.

The following analysis requires the concept of difference quotients. Given a function
u:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
x\in\Omega
,
h\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq l\leq n
, we put

\left(D^{h}_{l}u\right)(x):=\frac{u(x+he_{l})-u(x)}{h}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{when $x+he_{l}\in\Omega$}.

The following facts are readily available in the literature and we refer to Evans’ book
[
7
]
for a proof (see Chapter 5, Section 8, Subsection2, Theorem 3).

Lemma 2.2.2

(Properties of Difference Quotients)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a domain,
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\subset\Omega
and
1\leq l\leq n
.

(1)

There exists
h_{0}(\Omega,\Omega^{\prime})>0
such that
D^{h}_{l}u\in L^{2}(\Omega^{\prime})
for all
u\in W^{1,2}(\Omega)
and
0<|h|<h_{0}
. Additionally
\|D^{h}_{l}u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\prime})}\leq C\|\partial_{l}u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

(2)

If
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
\|D^{h}_{l}u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\prime})}\leq C<\infty
for all
0<|h|<h_{0}
, then
u
is weakly differentiable on
\Omega^{\prime}
with respect to
x_{l}
and
\|\partial_{l}u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\prime})}\leq C
.

We note that Lemma
2.2.2
also holds for domains in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)
and
1\leq l\leq n-1
.

Theorem 2.2.3

(Boundary Regularity)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)
be a bounded domain,
a^{ij}\in C^{1}(\bar{\Omega})
,
b^{i}
,
c\in C^{0}(\bar{\Omega})
and
\theta
,
\Lambda>0
such that
a^{ij}(x)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\geq\theta|\xi|^{2}
for all
x\in\Omega
and
\|a^{ij}\|_{C^{1}(\bar{\Omega})},

\|b^{i}\|_{C^{0}(\bar{\Omega})}
,
\|c\|_{C^{0}(\bar{\Omega})}\leq\Lambda
. Let further
f\in L^{2}(\Omega^{+})
,
g\in W^{1,2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)
and let
u\in W^{1,2}(\Omega^{+})
be a weak solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ l l}a^{ij}\partial_{ij}u+b^{i}\partial_{i}u+cu=f&\textrm{in $\Omega^{+}$},\\
-a^{nj}\partial_{j}u=g&\textrm{along $\partial^{+}\Omega$}.\end{array}\right.

Then
u\in W^{2,2}(V^{+})
for all
V\subset\subset\Omega
and

\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(V^{+})}\leq C(\Lambda,\theta,V,\Omega)(\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|g\|_{W^{1,2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)}).

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we will suppress the dependence of various constants on
\Lambda
and
\theta
. Let
W\subset\Omega
such that
V\subset\subset W\subset\subset\Omega
. For any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(W)
we have

\int_{\Omega^{+}}-a^{ij}\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}\varphi+\tilde{b}^{i}\partial_{i}u\varphi+cu\varphi=\int_{\Omega^{+}}f\varphi-\int_{\partial^{+}\Omega}g\varphi.

(2.2.2)

We choose a domain
\tilde{W}
such that
V\subset\subset\tilde{W}\subset\subset W
, a test function
\zeta\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that
\zeta\equiv 1
on
V
and
\zeta\equiv 0
on
\Omega\backslash\tilde{W}
and fix
1\leq l\leq n-1
. For small real numbers
|h|\leq h_{0}(V,W)
we consider the test function

\varphi:=D^{-h}_{l}\left(\zeta^{2}D_{l}^{h}u\right)\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{which, for $|h|\leq h_{0}$, satisfies }\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset W.

We insert this choice of
\varphi
into Equation (
2.2.2
) and investigate the individual terms by themselves. First, using a discrete integration by parts, we compute

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{+}}-a^{ij}\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}\varphi=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{+}}-a^{ij}\partial_{i}uD^{-h}_{l}\left[\partial_{j}\left(\zeta^{2}D^{h}_{l}u\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{+}}D^{h}_{l}(a^{ij}\partial_{i}u)\zeta^{2}D^{h}_{l}\left(\partial_{j}u\right)+\int_{\Omega^{+}}D^{h}_{l}(a^{ij}\partial_{i}u)2\zeta\partial_{j}\zeta D^{h}_{l}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{+}}a^{ij}(x+he_{l})\partial_{i}\left(D_{l}^{h}u\right)\zeta^{2}D^{h}_{l}\left(\partial_{j}u\right)+\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}D^{h}_{l}(a^{ij})\partial_{i}uD^{h}_{l}\left(\partial_{j}u\right)

\displaystyle+2\int_{\Omega^{+}}D^{h}_{l}(a^{ij})\partial_{i}u\zeta\partial_{j}\zeta D^{h}_{l}u+2\int_{\Omega^{+}}a^{ij}(x+he_{l})D^{h}_{l}(\partial_{i}u)\zeta\partial_{j}\zeta D^{h}_{l}u.

We can lower bound the first term by exploiting the uniform ellipticity of the coefficients
a^{ij}
. To get a lower term for the entire expression, we use the triangle inequality and the regularity of the coefficients to bound
|a^{ij}|+|D^{h}_{l}(a^{ij})|\leq C
. So

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{+}}-a^{ij}\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}\varphi\geq

\displaystyle\theta\int_{\Omega^{+}}|D^{h}_{l}\nabla u|^{2}\zeta^{2}-C\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}|\nabla u||D^{h}_{l}\nabla u|

\displaystyle-C\int_{\Omega^{+}}|\nabla\zeta|\zeta|\nabla u||D^{h}_{l}u|-C\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta|\nabla\zeta||D^{h}_{l}\nabla u||D^{h}_{l}u|.

Let
\epsilon>0
be a small parameter. Using Young’s inequality,
\operatorname{supp}(\zeta)\subset W
and
|\nabla\zeta|\leq C(V,W)
we estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{+}}-a^{ij}\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}\varphi\geq

\displaystyle\theta\int_{\Omega^{+}}|D^{h}_{l}\nabla u|^{2}\zeta^{2}-\epsilon\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}|D^{h}_{l}\nabla u|^{2}-C(\epsilon)\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}

\displaystyle-C\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta\left(|\nabla u|^{2}+|D^{h}_{l}u|^{2}\right)-C(\epsilon)\int_{\Omega^{+}}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}|D^{h}_{l}u|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(\theta-\epsilon)\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}|D^{h}_{l}\nabla u|^{2}-C(V,W,\epsilon)\int_{W^{+}}|\nabla u|^{2}

\displaystyle-C(V,W,\epsilon)\int_{\operatorname{supp}(\zeta)^{+}}|D^{h}_{l}u|^{2}.

(2.2.3)

For small enough
h
we may use
\operatorname{supp}(\zeta)\subset W
and Lemma
2.2.2
to estimate

\int_{\operatorname{supp}(\zeta)^{+}}|D^{h}_{l}u|^{2}\leq C\int_{W^{+}}|\nabla u|^{2}.

Inserting into Estimate (
2.2.3
) and choosing
\epsilon=\frac{1}{2}\theta
we obtain

\int_{\Omega^{+}}-a^{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\varphi\geq\frac{\theta}{2}\int_{\Omega^{+}}|D^{h}_{l}\nabla u|^{2}\zeta^{2}-C(V,W)\int_{W^{+}}|\nabla u|^{2}.

(2.2.4)

Using
\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)\subset W
, the continuity of
\tilde{b}^{i}
and
c
and Young’s inequality, we estimate

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega^{+}}f\varphi-\tilde{b}^{i}\partial_{i}u\varphi-cu\varphi\right|\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon\int_{\Omega^{+}}\varphi^{2}+C(\epsilon)\int_{W^{+}}|f|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2}+u^{2}.

(2.2.5)

Next, we estimate the
L^{2}
-norm of
\varphi
. Using
\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset W
, we may apply Lemma
2.2.2
for small enough
h
and estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{+}}\varphi^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{W^{+}}\left|D^{-h}_{l}\left(\zeta^{2}D_{l}^{h}u\right)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{W^{+}}\left|\partial_{l}\left(\zeta^{2}D_{l}^{h}u\right)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{W^{+}}|\zeta\nabla\zeta|^{2}|D^{h}_{l}u|^{2}+\zeta^{2}|D^{h}_{l}\nabla u|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(V,W)\left(\int_{W^{+}}|\nabla u|^{2}+\int_{W^{+}}\zeta^{2}|D^{h}_{l}\nabla u|^{2}\right).

(2.2.6)

Combining Estimates (
2.2.5
) and (
2.2.6
), we obtain

\left|\int_{\Omega^{+}}\hskip-2.84544ptf\varphi-\tilde{b}^{i}\partial_{i}u\varphi-cu\varphi\right|\leq C(\epsilon,V,W)\left(\int_{W^{+}}\hskip-2.84544pt|f|^{2}+|u|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2}\right)+\epsilon\int_{W^{+}}\hskip-2.84544pt\zeta^{2}|D^{h}_{l}\nabla u|^{2}.

(2.2.7)

Finally, we estimate the boundary integral in Equation (
2.2.2
). Since
\partial^{+}\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times 0
and
1\leq l\leq n-1
, we can move the difference quotient
D^{-h}_{l}
from the test function
\varphi
to
g
. Using
\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)\cap\partial^{+}\Omega\subset\partial^{+}W
and
g\in W^{1,2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)
we may then estimate

\left|\int_{\partial\Omega^{+}}gD^{-h}_{l}(\zeta^{2}D^{h}_{l}u)\right|=\left|\int_{\partial\Omega^{+}}(D^{h}_{l}g)\zeta^{2}D^{h}_{l}u\right|\leq C(\epsilon)\int_{\partial^{+}W}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}g|^{2}+\epsilon\int_{\partial^{+}\Omega}\zeta^{2}|D^{h}_{l}u|^{2}.

(2.2.8)

Since the trace operator
W^{1,2}(\Omega)\rightarrow L^{2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)
is continuous, we may estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}|D^{h}_{l}u|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle C\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}|D^{h}_{l}u|^{2}+\zeta|\nabla\zeta||D^{h}_{l}u|^{2}+\zeta^{2}|D^{h}_{l}u|\ |D^{h}_{l}\nabla u|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(V,W)\left(\int_{\operatorname{supp}(\zeta)^{+}}|D^{h}_{l}u|^{2}+\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}|D^{h}_{l}\nabla u|^{2}\right).

(2.2.9)

Inserting Estimate (
2.2.9
) into Estimate (
2.2.8
) and using Lemma
2.2.2
, we get

\left|\int_{\partial\Omega^{+}}gD^{-h}_{l}(\zeta^{2}D^{h}_{l}u)\right|\leq C(\epsilon,V,W)\left(\|g\|_{W^{1,2}(\partial^{+}W)}^{2}+\int_{W^{+}}|\nabla u|^{2}\right)+\epsilon\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}|D^{h}_{l}\nabla u|^{2}.

(2.2.10)

Putting Estimates (
2.2.4
), (
2.2.7
) and (
2.2.10
) into Equation (
2.2.2
) and choosing
\epsilon(\theta)
small enough, we obtain

\frac{\theta}{4}\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}|D^{h}_{l}\nabla u|^{2}\leq C(V,W)\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(W^{+})}^{2}+\|g\|_{W^{1,2}(\partial^{+}W)}^{2}+\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(W^{+})}^{2}\right).

(2.2.11)

As this estimate holds uniformly for all small
h
and
\zeta\equiv 1
on
V
, we deduce that on
V^{+}
the gradient
\nabla u
is weakly differentiable in
l
-direction and

\int_{V^{+}}|\partial_{l}\nabla u|^{2}\leq C(V,W)\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(W^{+})}^{2}+\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(W^{+})}^{2}+\|g\|_{W^{1,2}(\partial^{+}W)}^{2}\right).

(2.2.12)

Next, for
\epsilon>0
small we consider
V_{\epsilon}:=\set{x\in V\ }{\ x_{n}>\epsilon}
. By Theorem
2.2.1
, we deduce that
u\in W^{2,2}(V_{\epsilon})
for every small
\epsilon>0
. So, it suffices to derive an estimate for
\|\partial_{ni}u\|_{L^{2}(V_{\epsilon})}
that is independent of
\epsilon
. First, we note that for
1\leq i\leq n-1
we have
\|\partial_{ni}u\|_{L^{2}(V_{\epsilon})}\leq\|\partial_{i}\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(V^{+})}^{2}
, which is estimated by Estimate (
2.2.12
).
So it remains to establish a uniform estimate for
\|\partial_{nn}u\|_{L^{2}(V_{\epsilon})}
. As
u\in W^{2,2}(V_{\epsilon})
for all
\epsilon>0
, we deduce that pointwise almost everywhere in
V^{+}

\partial_{nn}u=\frac{1}{a^{nn}}\left(f-\sum_{i+j\leq 2n-1}a^{ij}\partial_{ij}u-\sum_{i=1}^{n}b^{i}\partial_{i}u-cu\right).

Again using Estimate (
2.2.12
), this implies the uniform estimate

\displaystyle\int_{V_{\epsilon}}|\partial_{nn}u|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle C(V,W)\left(\int_{V^{+}}f^{2}+\sum_{i+j\leq 2n-1}|\partial_{ij}u|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2}+u^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(V,W)\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(W^{+})}^{2}+\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(W^{+})}^{2}+\|g\|_{W^{1,2}(\partial^{+}W)}^{2}\right).

In total we have shown that
u\in W^{2,2}(V^{+})
and

\int_{V^{+}}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}\leq C(V,W)\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(W^{+})}^{2}+\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(W^{+})}^{2}+\|g\|_{W^{1,2}(\partial^{+}W)}^{2}\right).

(2.2.13)

This estimate is, however, worse than the one claimed in the theorem. We still need to derive an estimate for
\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(W^{+})}
. To do so, let
W\subset\subset Z\subset\subset\Omega
. Using the same arguments as before, we also may prove that
u\in W^{2,2}(W^{+})
. Now let
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega,[0,1])
be another cutoff function such that
\zeta\equiv 1
on
W
and
\zeta\equiv 0
outside of
Z
. Taking
\varphi=\zeta^{2}u
in Equation (
2.2.2
), we get

\int_{\Omega^{+}}a^{ij}\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}(\zeta^{2}u)=\int_{\Omega^{+}}(\tilde{b}^{i}\partial_{i}u+cu-f)\zeta^{2}u+\int_{\partial^{+}\Omega}g\zeta^{2}u.

(2.2.14)

We first estimate the term on the left-hand side by writing

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{+}}a^{ij}\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}(\zeta^{2}u)=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega+}\zeta^{2}a^{ij}\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}u+2\zeta a^{ij}\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}\zeta u

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\theta\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}-C(W,Z)\int_{Z^{+}}\zeta|\nabla u|\ |u|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(\theta-\epsilon)\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}-C(W,Z,\epsilon)\int_{Z^{+}}|u|^{2}.

(2.2.15)

Next, we estimate the right-hand side of Equation (
2.2.14
). Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, Young’s inequality and the continuity of the trace operator
W^{1,2}(\Omega^{+})\rightarrow L^{2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega^{+}}(\tilde{b}^{i}\partial_{i}u+cu-f)\zeta^{2}u+\int_{\partial^{+}\Omega}g\zeta^{2}u\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|\zeta\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}\right)\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)}\|\zeta u\|_{L^{2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|\zeta\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}\right)\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)}\|\zeta u\|_{W^{1,2}(\Omega^{+})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(\epsilon)\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}^{2}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)}^{2}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}^{2}\right)+\epsilon\|\zeta\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}^{2}+\epsilon\|\zeta u\|_{W^{1,2}(\Omega+)}^{2}.

(2.2.16)

We estimate

\|\zeta u\|_{W^{1,2}(\Omega^{+})}^{2}=\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}u^{2}+\int_{\Omega^{+}}(u\nabla\zeta+\zeta\nabla u)^{2}\leq C\int_{\Omega^{+}}u^{2}+\int_{\Omega^{+}}\zeta^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}.

Inserting into Estimate (
2.2.16
), we get

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega^{+}}(\tilde{b}^{i}\partial_{i}u+cu-f)\zeta^{2}u+\int_{\partial^{+}\Omega}g\zeta^{2}u\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(\epsilon)\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}^{2}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)}^{2}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}^{2}\right)+\epsilon\|\zeta\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}^{2}.

(2.2.17)

Putting Estimates (
2.2.15
) and (
2.2.17
) into Equation (
2.2.14
) and choosing
\epsilon(\theta)
small enough, we obtain

\int_{W^{+}}|\nabla u|^{2}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}^{2}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega^{+})}^{2}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}^{2}\right).

(2.2.18)

The theorem follows by combining Estimates (
2.2.13
) and (
2.2.18
).
∎

Next, we prove higher regularity.

Theorem 2.2.4

(Higher Boundary Regularity)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)
be a bounded domain,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
a^{ij}
,
b^{i}
,
c\in C^{m+1}(\bar{\Omega})
and
\theta
,
\Lambda>0
such that
a^{ij}(x)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\geq\theta|\xi|^{2}
for all
x\in\Omega
and
\|a^{ij}\|_{C^{m+1}(\bar{\Omega})}
,
\|b^{i}\|_{C^{m+1}(\bar{\Omega})}
,
\|c\|_{C^{m+1}(\bar{\Omega})}\leq\Lambda
. Let further
f\in W^{m,2}(\Omega^{+})
,
g\in W^{m+1,2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)
and let
u\in W^{1,2}(\Omega^{+})
be a weak solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ l l}a^{ij}\partial_{ij}u+b^{i}\partial_{i}u+cu=f&\textrm{in $\Omega^{+}$},\\
-a^{nj}\partial_{j}u=g&\textrm{along $\partial^{+}\Omega$}.\end{array}\right.

Then
u\in W^{m+2,2}(V^{+})
for any
V\subset\subset\Omega
and

\|u\|_{W^{m+2,2}(V^{+})}\leq C(\Lambda,\theta,V,\Omega)(\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|f\|_{W^{m,2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|g\|_{W^{m+1,2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)}).

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we will suppress the dependence of various constants on
\Lambda
,
\theta
,
V
and
\Omega
. The proof is inductive.
m=0
is taken care of in Theorem
2.2.3
. We prove
m\rightarrow m+1
. Let
V\subset\subset W\subset\subset\Omega
. As
f\in W^{m+1,2}(\Omega^{+})
, the inductive hypothesis implies
u\in W^{m+2,2}(W^{+})
and

\|u\|_{W^{m+2,2}(W^{+})}\leq C(W^{+},\Omega^{+})\left(\|f\|_{W^{m,2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|g\|_{W^{m+1,2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}\right).

(2.2.19)

Let
1\leq a\leq n-1
. It is easy to see that the function
\partial_{a}u
solves weakly

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}a^{ij}\partial_{ij}\left(\partial_{a}u\right)+b^{i}\partial_{i}\left(\partial_{a}u\right)+c\left(\partial_{a}u\right)=\partial_{a}f-\partial_{a}a^{ij}\partial_{ij}u-\partial_{a}b^{i}\partial_{i}u-\partial_{a}cu&\textrm{in $\Omega^{+}$,}\\
-a^{nj}\partial_{j}\left(\partial_{a}u\right)=\partial_{a}g+\partial_{a}a^{nj}\partial_{j}u&\textrm{along $\partial^{+}\Omega$.}\end{array}\right.

Applying the inductive hypothesis and Estimate (
2.2.19
) to this problem for
\partial_{a}u
and using Estimate (
2.2.19
), we deduce
\partial_{a}u\in W^{m+2,2}(V^{+})
and the estimate

\displaystyle\|\partial_{a}u\|_{W^{m+2,2}(V^{+})}\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\|\partial_{a}f\|_{W^{m,2}(W^{+})}+\|\partial_{a}g\|_{W^{m1,2}(\partial^{+}W)}+\|\partial_{a}u\|_{L^{2}(W^{+})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\|f\|_{W^{m+1,2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|g\|_{W^{m+2,2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}\right).

(2.2.20)

For small
\epsilon>0
we put
V_{\epsilon}:=\set{x\in V\ }{\ x_{n}>\epsilon}
. Using Theorem
2.2.1

we have
\partial_{n}u\in W^{m+2,2}(V_{\epsilon})
for all
\epsilon>0
. We aim for a uniform estimate for
\|\partial_{n}u\|_{W^{m+2,2}}
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
. To do this, we consider a multiindex
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})
of length
|\alpha|=m+2
. First we suppose
\alpha_{n}\leq m+1
, and without loss of generality
\alpha_{1}\neq 0
. We can use
\partial_{1}u\in W^{m+2,2}(V^{+})
, Schwarz’s theorem and Estimate (
2.2.20
) to deduce the uniform estimate

\|\nabla_{\alpha}\partial_{n}u\|_{L^{2}(V_{\epsilon})}\leq\|\partial_{1}u\|_{W^{m+2,2}(V^{+})}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{W^{m+1,2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|g\|_{W^{m+2,2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}\right).

So, we are left with estimating
\partial_{n}^{m+3}u
in
L^{2}(V_{\epsilon})
. As
u\in W^{2,2}(V^{+})
we deduce that pointwise, almost everywhere in
V^{+}

\partial_{nn}u=\frac{1}{a^{nn}}\left(f-\sum_{i+j\leq 2n-1}a^{ij}\partial_{ij}u-\sum_{i=1}^{n}b^{i}\partial_{i}u-cu\right).

(2.2.21)

Let
\epsilon>0
and
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(V_{\epsilon})
. We multiply Equation (
2.2.21
) with
(-1)^{m+1}\partial_{n}^{m+1}\varphi
and integrate. As
u\in W^{m+3,2}(V_{\epsilon})
we may integrate by parts to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{V^{+}}\partial_{n}^{m+3}u\varphi=

\displaystyle(-1)^{m+1}\int_{V^{+}}\partial_{n}^{2}u\partial_{n}^{m+1}\varphi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{m+1}\int_{V^{+}}\frac{1}{a^{nn}}\left(f-\sum_{i+j\leq 2n-1}a^{ij}\partial_{ij}u-\sum_{i=1}^{n}b^{i}\partial_{i}u-cu\right)\partial_{n}^{m+1}\varphi.

Now we use that
f\in W^{m+1,2}(\Omega^{+})
and that for all
1\leq i\leq n-1
we already have established that
\partial_{i}u\in W^{m+2,2}(V^{+})
as well as the smoothness of the coefficients to integrate by parts
m+1
-times. We then get that for all small
\epsilon>0
and
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(V_{\epsilon})
we have

\left|\int_{V_{\epsilon}}\partial_{n}^{m+3}u\varphi\right|\leq C\left(\|f\|_{W^{m+1,2}(\Omega^{+})}+\sum_{1=1}^{n-1}\|\partial_{i}u\|_{W^{m+2,2}(\Omega^{+})}\right)\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(V^{+}_{\epsilon})}.

By approximation, this estimate is valid for all
\varphi\in L^{2}(V_{\epsilon})
. In particular, as
\partial_{n}^{m+3}u\in L^{2}(V_{\epsilon})
we can take
\varphi=\partial_{n}^{m+3}u\in L^{2}(V_{\epsilon})
and find

\|\partial_{n}^{m+3}u\|_{L^{2}(V_{\epsilon})}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{W^{m+1,2}(\Omega^{+})}+\sum_{1=1}^{n-1}\|\partial_{i}u\|_{W^{m+2,2}(\Omega^{+})}\right).

(2.2.22)

Estimate (
2.2.22
) is uniform over all small
\epsilon>0
. Combining Estimates (
2.2.20
) and (
2.2.22
), we deduce
u\in W^{m+3,2}(V^{+})
and

\|u\|_{W^{m+3,2}(V^{+})}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{W^{m+1,2}(\Omega^{+})}+\|g\|_{W^{m+2,2}(\partial^{+}\Omega)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})}\right).

∎

2.3
Elliptic Regularity on Manifolds

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Let
\partial M\neq 0
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f\in W^{m,2}(M)
,
h\in W^{m+1,2}(\partial M)
and and
u\in W^{1,2}(M)
be a weak solution of

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta_{g}u&=f\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{in $M$},\\
\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}&=h\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{along $\partial M$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Then
u\in W^{m+2,2}(M)
and

\|u\|_{W^{m+2,2}(M)}\leq C(M,m,g)\left(\|f\|_{W^{m,2}(M)}+\|h\|_{W^{m+1,2}(\partial M)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}\right).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}=\set{(\varphi_{i},U_{i})\ }{\ 1\leq i\leq m}
be a good atlas. For each
1\leq i\leq m
let
\tilde{U}_{i}\supset\supset Ui
such that
\varphi_{i}
can be extended to a smooth map onto
\tilde{U}_{i}
and put
\tilde{V}_{i}:=\varphi_{i}(\tilde{U}_{i})
. We choose an open set
U_{i}^{\prime}
such that
U_{i}\subset\subset U_{i}^{\prime}\subset\subset\tilde{U}_{i}
, put
V_{i}^{\prime}:=\varphi_{i}(U_{i}^{\prime})
and let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:=\set{(\varphi_{i}^{\prime},U_{i}^{\prime})\ }{\ 1\leq i\leq m}
.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is also a good atlas. Finally, we let
u_{i}:=u\circ\varphi_{i}^{-1}
,
h_{i}:=h\circ\varphi_{i}^{-1}
and
f_{i}:=f\circ\varphi_{i}^{-1}
. For
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(V_{i}^{\prime})
, we have

\int_{M}g(\nabla_{g}u,\nabla_{g}(\psi\circ\varphi_{i}))d\mu_{g}=\int_{M}f\cdot(\psi\circ\varphi_{i})d\mu_{g}+\int_{\partial M}g\cdot(\psi\circ\varphi_{i})dS_{g}.

Expressing these integrals in the chart
V_{i}^{\prime}
and denoting the induced metric on the boundary by
\hat{g}
, we obtain

\int_{V_{i}^{\prime}}g^{ab}\partial_{a}u_{i}\partial_{b}\psi\sqrt{\det g}dx=\int_{V_{i}^{\prime}}f_{i}\psi\sqrt{\det g}dx+\int_{\partial^{+}V_{i}^{\prime}}h_{i}\psi\sqrt{\det{\hat{g}}}dS(x).

Here
dS(x)
is the
(n-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times 0
. The boundary integral is absent if
\varphi_{i}
is an interior chart. By Cramer’s rule
\sqrt{g^{nn}}\sqrt{\det g}=\sqrt{\det{\hat{g}}}
. So, we deduce that for each
1\leq i\leq m
,
u_{i}
is a weak solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}&\displaystyle-\left(g^{ab}\partial_{ab}-g^{ab}\Gamma^{k}_{\ ab}\partial_{k}\right)u_{i}=f_{i}&\displaystyle\textrm{in $V_{i}^{\prime}$,}\\
&\displaystyle-\frac{g^{na}}{\sqrt{g^{nn}}}\partial_{a}u_{i}=h_{i}&\displaystyle\textrm{along $\partial^{+}V_{i}^{\prime}$ if $U_{i}$ is a boundary chart.}\end{array}\right.

Using Theorem
2.2.1
or
2.2.4
, depending on whether
U_{i}
is a boundary chart or not, we deduce that
u_{i}\in W^{m+2,2}(V_{i})
with

\|u_{i}\|_{W^{m+2,2}(V_{i})}\leq C(V_{i},V_{i}^{\prime},m,g)\left(\|f_{i}\|_{W^{m,2}(V_{i}^{\prime})}+\|h_{i}\|_{W^{m+1,2}(\partial^{+}V_{i}^{\prime})}\right).

(2.3.1)

Here the last term on the right is absent if
U_{i}
is an interior chart.
Summing Estimate (
2.3.1
) over
1\leq i\leq m
, and using the equivalence of the Sobolev norms induced by the good atlases
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
from Proposition
1.4.4
, we deduce the theorem.
∎

If
\partial M=\emptyset
, we can follow the proof of Lemma
2.3.1
and obtain the following result.

Proposition 2.3.2
.

Let
\partial M=\emptyset
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f\in W^{m,2}(M)
and
u\in W^{1,2}(M)
be a weak solution of
-\Delta_{g}u=f
in
M
. Then
u\in W^{m+2,2}(M)
and

\|u\|_{W^{m+2,2}(M)}\leq C(M,m,g)\left(\|f\|_{W^{m,2}(M)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}\right).

As an easy consequence, we obtain the following lemma:

Lemma 2.3.3
.

Let
M
be a compact,
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with boundary
\partial M\neq\emptyset
and
k\geq 0
. If
u\in C^{2k+2}(M)
satisfies

\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu}\Delta_{g}^{l}u=0
for all
0\leq l\leq k
, then

\|u\|_{W^{2k,2}(M)}\leq C(M,g,k)\sum_{j=0}^{k}\|\Delta_{g}^{j}u\|_{L^{2}(M)}.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by an inductive-type argument over
k
. For
k=0
, the lemma is trivial. Now assume that the lemma is already proven for some
k
and let
u\in C^{2k+4}(M)
such that

\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu}\Delta_{g}^{l}u=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for all $0\leq l\leq k+1$}.

Note that we may apply the inductive hypothesis on the function
\Delta_{g}u\in C^{2k+2}(M)
. Additionally, we apply lemma
2.3.1
to the function
u
(note that
\partial_{\nu}u=0
). This gives the following two estimates:

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{g}u\|_{W^{2k,2}(M)}\leq

\displaystyle C(M,g,k)\sum_{j=0}^{k}\|\Delta_{g}^{j+1}u\|_{L^{2}(M)}

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{2k+2,2}(M)}\leq

\displaystyle C(M,g)\left(\|\Delta_{g}u\|_{W^{2k,2}(M)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}\right)

The lemma follows by combining these two estimates.
∎

Corollary 2.3.4
.

Let
(M,g)
be a compact,
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with
\partial M\neq\emptyset
,
\lambda\geq 0
and
u\in W^{1,2}(M)
be a weak solution of

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta_{g}u&=\lambda u\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{in $M$},\\
\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}&=0\hskip 21.33955pt\textrm{along $\partial M$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Then
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
and

\|u\|_{W^{2m,2}(M)}\leq C(M,g,m)(1+\lambda^{m})\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}.

Proof.

If
u\in W^{k,2}(M)
for some
k\geq 1
, then, using Theorem
2.3.1
, we get
u\in W^{k+2,2}(M)
. Since
u\in W^{1,2}(M)
, we deduce
u\in\bigcap_{l\geq 0}W^{l,2}(M)
and hence, by Sobolev embedding,
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Therefore we may compute
\partial_{\nu}\Delta_{g}^{k}u=(-1)^{k}\lambda^{k}\partial_{\nu}u=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Applying Lemma
2.3.3
, we get

\|u\|_{W^{2m,2}(M)}\leq C(M,g,m)\sum_{j=0}^{m}\lambda^{j}\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}\leq C(M,g,m)(m+1)\max(1,\lambda^{m})\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}.

∎

Combining the results from Lemma
2.1.2
and Corollary
2.3.4
, we get the following theorem.

Theorem 2.3.5

(Spectral Decomposition of the Neumann Laplacian)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a smooth compact,
n
-dimensional connected Riemannian manifold. There exists
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq...\leq\lambda_{k}\rightarrow\infty
and smooth
L^{2}_{g}(M)
-orthonormal and
W^{1,2}_{g}(M)
-orthogonal functions
\phi_{k}\in C^{\infty}(M)
that satisfy

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rll}\displaystyle-\Delta_{g}\phi_{k}&\displaystyle=\lambda_{k}\phi_{k}&\displaystyle\textrm{in $M$},\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial\phi_{k}}{\partial\nu}&\displaystyle=0&\displaystyle\textrm{along $\partial M$}.\end{array}\right.

The span of the functions
\phi_{k}
is dense in
L^{2}(M)
and
W^{1,2}(M)
. Additionally,
\phi_{0}=|M|^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and

\|\phi_{k}\|_{W^{2m,2}(M)}\leq C(M,m)(1+\lambda_{k}^{m}).

3
|
Schauder Estimates in
\mathbb{R}^{n}

3.1
Liouville Type Theorems

Throughout this section, we fix a strongly elliptic matrix
\left(a^{ij}\right)_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
. That is there exists
\theta\in(0,\infty)
such that

\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\ a^{ij}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\geq\theta|\xi|^{2}.

Throughout this section, we study

\displaystyle\dot{u}+a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}u=0,

(3.1.1)

\displaystyle a^{ni}\partial_{i}u=a^{ni}a^{kl}\partial_{ikl}u=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{along $x_{n}=0$},

(3.1.2)

on various domains and prove that solutions must be polynomials if they satisfy suitable growth estimates.

Definition 3.1.1

(Parabolic Polynomials)
.

A function
p:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is called
parabolic polynomial
of
parabolic degree

\operatorname{deg}(p)=d
, if

p(x,t)=\sum_{|\alpha|+4j\leq d}a_{\alpha,j}x^{\alpha}t^{j}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for real parameters }a_{\alpha,j}.

Definition 3.1.2

(Parabolic Metric)
.

The parabolic metric
d:(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})\times(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is defined as
d((x,t),(y,s)):=\max(|x-y|,|t-s|^{\frac{1}{4}})
.

Note that the parabolic balls
U_{\rho}(p)
from Definition
1.2.1
are precisely the metric balls with respect to the parabolic metric.

Theorem 3.1.3
.

Let
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0])
satisfy
\dot{u}=-a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}u
as well as
|u(x,t)|\leq C(1+d((x,t),0))^{4+\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
C>0
and all
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]
. Then
u
is a parabolic polynomial of parabolic degree
\deg(u)\leq 4
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we will suppress the dependence of various constants on
a^{ij}
and
\theta
. Let
0<\rho_{1}<{\rho_{2}}
and
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(U_{\rho_{2}}(0),[0,1])
such that
\eta\equiv 1
on
U_{\rho_{1}}^{-}(0)
. We compute

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]}\eta^{4}u(\dot{u}+a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}u)dxdt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]}\eta^{4}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(u^{2})dxdt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]}\eta^{4}ua^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}udxdt.

(3.1.3)

We first simplify the first integral. Since
\operatorname{supp}(\eta)\subset U_{\rho_{2}}(0)
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{4}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(u^{2})dxdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\rho_{2}}(0)}\left[\int_{-\rho_{2}^{4}}^{0}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(\eta^{4}u^{2})dt\right]dx-\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}4\eta^{3}\dot{\eta}u^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2}(0)}\eta^{4}(x,0)^{2}u(x,0)^{2}-\eta(x,-\rho_{2}^{4})^{4}u(x,-\rho_{2}^{4})^{2}dx-\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}4\eta^{3}\dot{\eta}u^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2}(0)}\eta^{4}(x,0)^{2}u(x,0)^{2}dx-\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}4\eta^{3}\dot{\eta}u^{2}dxdt.

(3.1.4)

The last step is justified as
\eta
has compact support in
U_{\rho_{2}}(0)
. Next, we simplify the second integral in Equation (
3.1.3
). Since
\eta\equiv 0
on
\partial B_{\rho_{2}}(0)\times[-\rho_{2}^{4},\rho_{2}^{4}]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{4}ua^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}udxdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}a^{ij}\partial_{ij}\left(\eta^{4}u\right)a^{kl}\partial_{kl}udxdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{4}a^{ij}\partial_{ij}u\,a^{kl}\partial_{kl}udxdt+\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}a^{ij}(u\partial_{ij}(\eta^{4})+8\eta^{3}\partial_{i}\eta\partial_{j}u)a^{kl}\partial_{kl}udxdt.

(3.1.5)

Inserting Equations (
3.1.4
) and (
3.1.5
) into Equation (
3.1.3
) and rearranging, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{4}a^{ij}\partial_{ij}ua^{kl}\partial_{kl}udxdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}a^{ij}(u\partial_{ij}(\eta^{4})+8\eta^{3}\partial_{i}\eta\partial_{j}u)a^{kl}\partial_{kl}udxdt

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\rho_{2}}(0)}\eta^{4}(x,0)^{2}u(x,0)^{2}dx+\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}2\eta^{3}\dot{\eta}u^{2}dxdt.

(3.1.6)

First note that
-\int_{B_{\rho_{2}}(0)}\eta^{4}(x,0)^{2}u(x,0)^{2}dx\leq 0
and can therefore be dropped in favor of an estimate. It is precisely this step where the same proof breaks down if we were to work on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[0,\infty)
instead. Additionally, we estimate

\left|\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}2\eta^{3}\dot{\eta}u^{2}\right|\leq C(\eta)\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}u^{2}.

(3.1.7)

Let
\epsilon>0
be a small but arbitrary parameter. Using Young’s inequality, we estimate

\displaystyle\left|\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}a^{ij}u\partial_{ij}(\eta^{4})a^{kl}\partial_{kl}udxdt\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}a^{ij}u\left(4\eta^{3}\partial_{ij}\eta+12\eta^{2}\partial_{i}\eta\partial_{j}\eta\right)a^{kl}\partial_{kl}udxdt\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}a^{ij}u\left(4\eta\partial_{ij}\eta+12\partial_{i}\eta\partial_{j}\eta\right)\left(\eta^{2}a^{kl}\partial_{kl}u\right)dxdt\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(\eta,\epsilon)\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}u^{2}dxdt+\epsilon\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{4}\left(a^{ij}\partial_{ij}u\right)^{2}dxdt.

(3.1.8)

Using the same arguments, we estimate

\displaystyle\left|\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}a^{ij}\eta^{3}\partial_{i}\eta\partial_{j}ua^{kl}\partial_{kl}udxdt\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}a^{ij}\eta\partial_{i}\eta\partial_{j}u\left(\eta^{2}a^{kl}\partial_{kl}u\right)dxdt\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(\epsilon)\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\left(a^{ij}\eta\partial_{i}\eta\partial_{j}u\right)^{2}+\epsilon\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\left(\eta^{2}a^{kl}\partial_{kl}u\right)^{2}dxdt.

(3.1.9)

Putting Estimates (
3.1.7
), (
3.1.8
) and (
3.1.9
) into Estimate (
3.1.6
) and choosing
\epsilon>0
small enough, we obtain

\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\left(\eta^{2}a^{kl}\partial_{kl}u\right)^{2}dxdt\leq C(\eta)\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\hskip 0.0ptu^{2}dxdt+C(\eta)\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\hskip 0.0pt\eta^{2}\left(a^{ij}\eta\partial_{i}\eta\partial_{j}u\right)^{2}dxdt.

(3.1.10)

We further estimate the second integral. Let
A:=(a^{ij})_{ij}
.
A
is a positive, symmetric matrix and hence
\langle x,y\rangle_{A}:=a^{ij}x_{i}y_{j}
is a scalar product. Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we estimate

\left(a^{ij}\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}\eta\right)^{2}=\langle\nabla u,\nabla\eta\rangle_{A}^{2}\leq\langle\nabla u,\nabla u\rangle_{A}\langle\nabla\eta,\nabla\eta\rangle_{A}=\left(a^{ij}\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}u\right)\left(a^{kl}\partial_{k}\eta\partial_{l}\eta\right).

(3.1.11)

We multiply Estimate (
3.1.11
) by
\eta^{2}
and integrate to get

\displaystyle\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{2}\left(a^{ij}\partial_{i}\eta\partial_{j}u\right)^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{2}\left(a^{ij}\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}u\right)\left(a^{kl}\partial_{k}\eta\partial_{l}\eta\right)dxdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}u\partial_{i}\left(\eta^{2}a^{ij}\partial_{j}ua^{kl}\partial_{k}\eta\partial_{l}\eta\right)dxdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{2}a^{ij}a^{kl}\left(u\partial_{ij}u\partial_{k}\eta\partial_{l}\eta+2u\partial_{j}u\partial_{ik}\eta\partial_{l}\eta\right)dxdt

\displaystyle-2\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}(0)}u\eta\partial_{i}\eta a^{ij}\partial_{j}ua^{kl}\partial_{k}\eta\partial_{l}\eta dxdt.

(3.1.12)

We first focus on the second integral. Let
\epsilon>0
be a small parameter. Using Young’s inequality, we estimate

\displaystyle\left|\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}(0)}u\eta\partial_{i}\eta a^{ij}\partial_{j}ua^{kl}\partial_{k}\eta\partial_{l}\eta dxdt\right|\leq

\displaystyle\left|\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}(0)}(\eta a^{ij}\partial_{i}\eta\partial_{j}u)(ua^{kl}\partial_{k}\eta\partial_{l}\eta)dxdt\right|

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\leq\epsilon\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}(0)}\eta^{2}(a^{ij}\partial_{i}\eta\partial_{j}u)^{2}dxdt+C(\eta,\epsilon)\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}(0)}u^{2}dxdt.

Inserting into Estimate (
3.1.12
) and choosing
\epsilon>0
small enough yields

\displaystyle\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{2}\left(a^{ij}\partial_{i}\eta\partial_{j}u\right)^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{2}a^{ij}a^{kl}\left(u\partial_{ij}u\partial_{k}\eta\partial_{l}\eta+2u\partial_{j}u\partial_{ik}\eta\partial_{l}\eta\right)dxdt+C(\eta)\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}(0)}u^{2}dxdt.

(3.1.13)

Let
\epsilon>0
denote a small parameter. Using Young’s inequality, we estimate

\left|\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\hskip-2.84544pt\eta^{2}a^{ij}\partial_{ij}ua^{kl}\partial_{k}\eta\partial_{l}\eta udxdt\right|\leq\epsilon\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\hskip-5.69046pt\left(\eta^{2}a^{ij}\partial_{ij}u\right)^{2}dxdt+C(\eta,\epsilon)\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\hskip-8.5359ptu^{2}dxdt.

(3.1.14)

Next, we write
2u\partial_{j}u=\partial_{j}(u^{2})
. This allows us to perform another partial integration and obtain

\displaystyle\left|-2\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{2}a^{ij}a^{kl}u\partial_{j}u\partial_{ik}\eta\partial_{l}\eta dxdt\right|=

\displaystyle\left|-\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{2}a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{j}(u^{2})\partial_{ik}\eta\partial_{l}\eta dxdt\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\partial_{j}\left(\eta^{2}a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ik}\eta\partial_{l}\eta\right)(u^{2})dxdt\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(\eta)\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}u^{2}dxdt.

(3.1.15)

Putting Estimates (
3.1.14
) and (
3.1.15
) into Estimate (
3.1.13
) and subsequently into Estimate (
3.1.10
) and choosing
\epsilon>0
small enough, we obtain

\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\left(\eta^{2}a^{kl}\partial_{kl}u\right)^{2}dxdt\leq C(\eta)\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}u^{2}dxdt.

(3.1.16)

The matrix
A=\left(a^{ij}\right)_{ij}
is positive and symmetric and therefore has a positive and symmetric square root
B:=\left(b^{ij}\right)_{ij}
– that is
B^{2}=A
. We estimate

a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ik}u\partial_{jl}u=a^{ij}b^{kc}b^{lc}\partial_{ik}u\partial_{jl}u=a^{ij}\partial_{i}(b^{kc}\partial_{k}u)\partial_{j}(b^{lc}\partial_{l}u).

As
a^{ij}
is elliptic with ellipticity constant
\theta>0
, we get

a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ik}u\partial_{jl}u\geq\theta\sum_{c=1}^{n}|\nabla(b^{kc}\partial_{k}u)|^{2}=\theta\sum_{c,s=1}^{n}|(b^{kc}\partial_{ks}u)|^{2}.

Note that for fixed indices
s
and
c
we have
(b^{kc}\partial_{ks}u)=(B\nabla^{2}u)^{c}_{\ s}
and hence

a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ik}u\partial_{jl}u\geq\theta\sum_{c,s=1}^{n}(B\nabla^{2}u)^{c}_{\ s}(B\nabla^{2}u)^{c}_{\ s}=\theta\operatorname{tr}(\left(B\nabla^{2}u\right)^{T}B\nabla^{2}u)=\theta\operatorname{tr}(\nabla^{2}uA\nabla^{2}u).

Finally, we need to compute
\operatorname{tr}(\nabla^{2}uA\nabla^{2}u)
. Using the ellipticity of
a^{ij}
, we obtain

a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ik}u\partial_{jl}u\geq\theta\sum_{k=1}^{n}a^{ij}\partial_{ik}u\partial_{jk}u\geq\theta^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}|\partial_{k}\nabla u|^{2}=\theta^{2}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}.

(3.1.17)

Using
\eta\equiv 1
on
U_{\rho_{1}}^{-}(0)
and Estimate (
3.1.17
), we can use Estimate (
3.1.16
) to deduce

\int_{U_{\rho_{1}}^{-}(0)}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}dxdt\leq\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{4}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}dxdt\leq C(\eta)\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}u^{2}dxdt.

(3.1.18)

Note that
|\nabla u|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\Delta(u^{2})-u\Delta u
. Using this identity and partial integration, we may estimate

\displaystyle\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{4}|\nabla u|^{2}dxdt=

\displaystyle\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{4}\left(\frac{1}{2}\Delta(u^{2})-u\Delta u\right)dxdt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\Delta(\eta^{4})u^{2}dxdt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{4}u^{2}dxdt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{4}(\Delta u)^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(\eta)\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}u^{2}dxdt+C\int_{U_{\rho_{2}}^{-}(0)}\eta^{2}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}dxdt.

Combining this estimate with Estimate (
3.1.18
), we deduce that for
k=0,1,2

\int_{U_{\rho_{1}}^{-}(0)}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}dxdt\leq C(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},k,\eta)\int_{U_{{\rho_{2}}}^{-}(0)}u^{2}dxdt.

This estimate is valid for any solution of
\dot{u}+a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}u=0
. In particular, it is also valid for
u\rightarrow\nabla^{m}u
. Using an inductive argument and suitable choices for
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
, we deduce that

\int_{U_{1}^{-}(0)}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}dxdt\leq C(k)\int_{U_{2}^{-}(0)}u^{2}dxdt.

(3.1.19)

Note that by
\partial_{t}u=-a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}u
, we can trade a time derivative for a fourth-order spatial derivative. Therefore we may use Estimate (
3.1.19
) and obtain

\int_{U_{1}^{-}(0)}|\nabla^{k}\partial_{t}^{l}u|^{2}dxdt\leq C(k,l)\int_{U_{2}^{-}(0)}u^{2}dxdt\leq C(k,l)\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(U_{2}^{-}(0))}^{2}.

(3.1.20)

Estimate (
3.1.20
) is valid for all solutions of
\dot{v}+a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}v=0
. In particular, taking
R>1
and putting
u_{R}(x,t):=u(Rx,R^{4}t)
, Estimate (
3.1.20
) is also valid for
u_{R}
. Hence

C(k,l)\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(U_{2R}^{-}(0))}=C(k,l)\|u_{R}\|_{L^{\infty}(U_{2}^{-}(0))}\geq\|\nabla^{k}\partial_{t}^{l}u_{R}\|_{L^{2}(U_{1}^{-}(0))}=\frac{R^{4l+k}}{R^{\frac{n+4}{2}}}\|\partial_{t}^{l}\nabla^{k}u\|_{L^{2}(U_{R}^{-}(0))}.

Letting
R\rightarrow\infty
, this shows that
\partial_{t}^{l}\nabla^{k}u\equiv 0
for
4l+k>\frac{n}{2}+2
and hence
u
is a parabolic polynomial. Due to the growth estimate
|u(x,t)|\leq C(1+d((x,t),0))^{4+\gamma}
we deduce that
u
must have parabolic degree 4 or less.
∎

We can extend this theorem to the situation where
u
is not smooth by using convolution. Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
be a smooth function with values in
[0,1]
,
\operatorname{supp}(\phi)\subset B_{1}(0)\times(-1,1)
and

\int_{B_{1}(0)\times(-1,1)}\phi(y,s)dyds=1.

For
\epsilon>0
we define
\phi_{\epsilon}(y,s):=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{4+n}}\phi\left(\frac{y}{\epsilon},\frac{s}{\epsilon^{4}}\right)
. As usual, the mollification of a function
v
is defined as

(\phi_{\epsilon}*v)(x,t):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}}\phi_{\epsilon}(x-y,t-s)v(y,s)dyds.

We require the following standard lemma:

Lemma 3.1.4
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
be open and
u\in C^{0}(\Omega)
. Let
\Omega_{\epsilon}:=\set{p\in\Omega\ }{\ U_{\epsilon}(p)\subset\Omega}
. Then
\phi_{\epsilon}*u\in C^{\infty}(\Omega_{\epsilon})
and
\phi_{\epsilon}*u\rightarrow u
in
C^{0}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega)
. Additionally,
\partial_{t}^{k}\nabla_{\alpha}u_{\epsilon}=\phi_{\epsilon}*(\partial_{t}^{k}\nabla_{\alpha}u)
if
\partial_{t}^{k}\nabla_{\alpha}u
exists and is continuous.

Corollary 3.1.5
.

Let
u\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0])
satisfy Equation (
3.1.1
) on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]
as well as
|u(x,t)|\leq C(1+d((x,t),0))^{4+\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
C>0
and all
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]
. Then
u
is a parabolic polynomial of parabolic degree
\operatorname{deg}(u)\leq 4
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. For
\delta<\epsilon
we consider the convolution
u_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,-\epsilon]\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\ u_{\delta}(x,t):=(\phi_{\delta}*u)(x,t)
. By Lemma
3.1.4

u_{\delta}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,-\epsilon])
and satisfies
\dot{u}_{\delta}=-a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}u_{\delta}
. Additionally, since
\delta\in(0,1)
, we may estimate

\displaystyle|u_{\delta}(x,t)|\leq

\displaystyle C\int_{U_{\delta}(0)}\phi_{\delta}(y,s)(1+d((x,t)-(y,s),0))^{4+\gamma}dyds

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\int_{U_{\delta}(0)}\phi_{\delta}(y,s)\left(1+d((x,t),0)\right)^{4+\gamma}dyds

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(1+d((x,t),0))^{4+\gamma}.

So, by Theorem
3.1.3
,
u_{\delta}
is a parabolic polynomial of degree at most 4 for all
\delta<\epsilon
. That is

u_{\delta}(x,t)=a(\delta)t+\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 4}b_{\alpha}(\delta)x^{\alpha}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for all $x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$ and $t\leq-\epsilon$.}

Noting the formulas

\displaystyle b_{\alpha}(\delta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha!}\nabla_{\alpha}u_{\delta}(0,-2)=\frac{1}{\alpha!}\int_{U_{\delta}(0)}\phi_{\delta}(y,s)\nabla_{\alpha}u(0-y,-2-s)dyds,

\displaystyle a(\delta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u_{\delta}(0,t)\bigg|_{t=-2}=\int_{U_{\delta}(0)}\phi_{\delta}(y,s)\dot{u}(0-y,-2-s)dyds,

it is readily seen that
b_{\alpha}(\delta)\rightarrow\frac{1}{\alpha!}\nabla_{\alpha}u(0,-2)=:b_{\alpha}^{*}
and
a(\delta)\rightarrow\dot{u}(0,-2)=:a^{*}
as
\delta\rightarrow 0^{+}
. Consequently
u_{\delta}\rightarrow a^{*}t+\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 4}b^{*}_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}
in
C^{0}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,-\epsilon])
. However, we also know that
u_{\delta}\rightarrow u
in
C^{0}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,-\epsilon))
. Therefore

u(x,t)=a^{*}t+\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 4}b^{*}_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for all }(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,-\epsilon).

Since this is true for all
\epsilon>0
, the corollary follows.
∎

Corollary 3.1.6
.

Let
u\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
satisfy Equation (
3.1.1
) on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
as well as
|u(x,t)|\leq C(1+d((x,t),0))^{4+\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
C>0
and all
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
. Then
u
is a parabolic polynomial of parabolic degree
\operatorname{deg}(u)\leq 4
.

Proof.

For
T>0
, we put
v_{T}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
v_{T}(x,t):=u(x,t+T)
. It is easy to see that Corollary
3.1.5
is applicable and hence,
v_{T}
is a parabolic polynomial of degree at most 4. Consequently there exist
a(T),b_{\alpha}(T)\in\mathbb{R}
such that

u(x,t)=v(x,t-T)=a(T)t+\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 4}b_{\alpha}(T)x^{\alpha}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for all }(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,T].

This formula implies
a(T)=\dot{u}(0,0)
and
b_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\alpha!}\nabla_{\alpha}u(0,0)
and so the coefficients
a(T)
and
b_{\alpha}(T)
do not depend on
T
. Letting
T\rightarrow\infty
, we deduce the corollary.
∎

Corollary 3.1.7
.

Let
u\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[0,\infty))
satisfy Equation (
3.1.1
) on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[0,\infty)
and as well as
|u(x,t)|\leq C(1+d((x,t),0))^{4+\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
C>0
and all
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[0,\infty)
. If
u(\cdot,0)
is a polynomial of degree 4 or less, then
u
is a parabolic polynomial of parabolic degree
\operatorname{deg}(u)\leq 4
.

Proof.

We first assume that
u(\cdot,0)=0
. In this case, we define a new function
v:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by mapping
v(x,t):=u(x,t)
for
t\geq 0
and
v(x,t):=0
for
t<0
. We claim that
v\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
. Suppose for the moment this is already shown. Then clearly
\dot{v}+a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}v=0
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
and still
|v(x,t)|\leq C(1+d((x,t),0))^{4+\gamma}
. So, by Corollary
3.1.6
, the corollary follows.

To prove
v\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
, it suffices to check that for each
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
the function
v
is
C^{4,1}
near
(x_{0},0)
. To see this first note that
v(x_{0}+\xi,0)\equiv 0
, so at
(x_{0},0)
the function
v
is four times differentiable in space with
\nabla^{k}v(x_{0},0)=0
. For
\epsilon>0
we compute

\frac{v(x_{0},\epsilon)-v(x_{0},0)}{\epsilon}=\frac{u(x_{0},\epsilon)-u(x_{0},0)}{\epsilon}=\int_{0}^{1}\dot{u}(x_{0},s\epsilon)ds\rightarrow\dot{u}(x_{0},0)=a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}u(x_{0},0)=0.

Also, since
v(x_{0},-\epsilon)=v(x_{0},0)=0
we have
\frac{v(x_{0},-\epsilon)-v(x_{0},0)}{-\epsilon}\equiv 0
.
So, with respect to time
v
is differentiable at
(x_{0},0)
and
\dot{v}(x_{0},0)=0
.

It remains to check that the partial derivatives are continuous. Again it suffices to check this at points
(x_{0},0)
. First we use that
u\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[0,\infty))
to deduce

\nabla^{k}v(x_{0},0)=\nabla^{k}u(x_{0},0)=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x,t)\rightarrow(x_{0},0)\\
t>0\end{subarray}}\nabla^{k}u(x,t)=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x,t)\rightarrow(x_{0},0)\\
t>0\end{subarray}}\nabla^{k}v(x,t).

Next we note that
\nabla^{k}v(x_{0},0)=0
and as
v\equiv 0
for
t<0
we clearly also have

\nabla^{k}v(x,t)\rightarrow\nabla^{k}v(x_{0},0)
when
(x,t)\rightarrow(x_{0},0)
and
t<0
. For the time derivative, we argue similarly.
\dot{v}(x_{0},0)=0
and hence it is clear that
\dot{v}(x,t)\rightarrow\dot{v}(x_{0},0)
when
(x,t)\rightarrow(x_{0},0)
and
t<0
. For
t>0
we have
\dot{v}(x,t)=\dot{u}(x,t)=-a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}u(x,t)
. Letting
(x,t)\rightarrow(x_{0},0)
we use
u\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[0,\infty))
to deduce

a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}u(x,t)\rightarrow a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}u(x_{0},0)=0
. The last step is justified by
u(x,0)\equiv 0
. So
\dot{v}(x,t)\rightarrow\dot{v}(x_{0},0)
when
(x,t)\rightarrow(x_{0},0)
and
t>0
.

Finally, we justify that we can assume
u(\cdot,0)=0
. Indeed, in the general situation, we define

p(x):=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 4}\frac{\nabla_{\alpha}u(0,0)}{\alpha!}x^{\alpha}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and put}\hskip 14.22636pt\tilde{u}(x,t):=u(x,t)-p(x)+ta^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}p(x).

\tilde{u}-u
is a parabolic polynomial with
\operatorname{deg}(\tilde{u}-u)\leq 4
. In particular,
\tilde{u}
satisfies the same growth estimate as
u
and if
\tilde{u}
is a parabolic polynomial with degree
\operatorname{deg}(\tilde{u})\leq 4
then
u
is as well. We compute

\dot{\tilde{u}}+a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}\tilde{u}=\dot{u}+a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}p+a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}(u-p)=0.

Since
u(\cdot,0)
is a polynomial of degree
4
or less, the same is true for
\tilde{u}
. Since additionally
\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{u}(0,0)=0
for all
|\alpha|\leq 4
, we deduce that

\tilde{u}(x,0)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}:\ |\alpha|\leq 4}\frac{\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{u}(0,0)}{\alpha!}x^{\alpha}=0.

So, by the special case considered in the beginning of the proof,
\tilde{u}
is a parabolic polynomial of degree
\operatorname{deg}(\tilde{u})\leq 4
, which implies the corollary.
∎

It is possible to extend these Liouville type theorems to the situation where Equation (
3.1.1
) is not satisfied on all of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
but only on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)
when the boundary conditions (
3.1.2
) are imposed. The fundamental observation is the following lemma:

Lemma 3.1.8
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval and
u\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times I)
satisfy Equation (
3.1.1
) and the boundary conditions from Equation (
3.1.2
). Let
v:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a^{ni}}{a^{nn}}e_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rll}\tilde{u}(x,t)&:=u(x,t)&\textrm{if $x_{n}\geq 0$},\\
\tilde{u}(x,t)&:=u(x-2x_{n}v,t)&\textrm{if $x_{n}<0$}.\\
\end{array}\right.

Then
\tilde{u}\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times I)
and
\tilde{u}
is a classical solution to Equation (
3.1.1
).

Proof.

It is clear that
\tilde{u}\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times I)
and
\tilde{u}\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(-\infty,0]\times I)
. We may deduce
\tilde{u}\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times I)
once we have shown that for
0\leq k\leq 4

\lim_{x_{n}\rightarrow 0^{-}}\partial_{n}^{k}\tilde{u}(x,t)=\lim_{x_{n}\rightarrow 0^{+}}\partial_{n}^{k}\tilde{u}(x,t).

(3.1.21)

Clearly
\tilde{u}
solves Equation (
3.1.1
) on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times I
. We now prove, that it also satisfies (
3.1.1
) on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(-\infty,0]\times I
. For
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times I
with
x_{n}<0
we compute

\partial_{i}\tilde{u}(x,t)=\partial_{a}u(x-2x_{n}v,t)\left(\delta^{i}_{a}-2\delta^{i}_{n}v_{a}\right).

(3.1.22)

Using
v_{i}=\frac{a^{ni}}{a^{nn}}
, we compute

\displaystyle a^{ij}\left(\delta^{i}_{a}-2\delta^{i}_{n}v_{a}\right)\left(\delta^{j}_{b}-2\delta^{j}_{n}v_{b}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(a^{aj}-2a^{nj}v_{a}\right)\left(\delta^{j}_{b}-2\delta^{j}_{n}v_{b}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a^{ab}-2a^{an}v_{b}-2a^{nb}v_{a}+4a^{nn}v_{a}v_{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a^{ab}-2a^{an}\frac{a^{nb}}{a^{nn}}-2a^{nb}\frac{a^{na}}{a^{nn}}+4a^{nn}\frac{a^{na}a^{nb}}{(a^{nn})^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a^{ab}.

Combining with Equation (
3.1.22
), we obtain

a^{ij}\partial_{ij}\tilde{u}(x,t)=a^{ab}\partial_{ab}u(x-2x_{n}v,t)
and hence

\dot{\tilde{u}}(x,t)+a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}\tilde{u}(x,t)=(\dot{u}+a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}u)(x-2x_{n}v,t)=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{in }\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(-\infty,0]\times I.

It remains to establish Equation (
3.1.21
). The case
k=0
is trivial. Indeed, if
x_{n}\rightarrow 0^{-}
we get

\tilde{u}((\vec{x},x_{n}),t)=u((\vec{x}-2x_{n}\vec{v},-x_{n}),t)\rightarrow u((\vec{x},0),t)
. Next, we consider

\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}\tilde{u}((\vec{x},x_{n}),t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}u(x-2x_{n}v,t)=\frac{\partial u}{\partial x_{n}}(x-2x_{n}v,t)-2\langle\nabla u(x-2x_{n}v,t),v\rangle.

As
x_{n}\rightarrow 0^{-}
, the first term converges to
\partial_{n}u((\vec{x},0),t)
. Using the first order boundary condition from Equation (
3.1.2
), the second term converges to

-2\langle\nabla u((\vec{x},0),t),v\rangle)=-2\frac{a^{ni}\partial_{i}u((\vec{x},0),t)}{a^{nn}}=0.

For the second derivative, we argue similarly. For
x=(\vec{x},x_{n})
with
x_{n}<0
we compute

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\tilde{u}}{\partial x_{n}^{2}}(x,t)

\displaystyle=D^{2}u(x-2x_{n}v,t)[e_{n}-2v,e_{n}-2v]

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x_{n}^{2}}(x-2x_{n}v,t)+4\left[D^{2}u(x-2x_{n}v)[v,v]-D^{2}u(x-2x_{n}v)[v,e_{n}]\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x_{n}^{2}}(x-2x_{n}v,t)+4D^{2}u(x-2x_{n}v)[v,v-e_{n}].

When
x_{n}\rightarrow 0^{-}
, the first term converges to
\partial_{n}^{2}u((\vec{x},0),t)
. We put
v^{\parallel}:=v-e_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times 0
. Differentiating the first order boundary condition in Equation (
3.1.2
) in direction
v^{\parallel}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{n}\rightarrow 0^{-}}4D^{2}u(x-2x_{n}v)[v,v-e_{n}]=

\displaystyle 4D^{2}u((\vec{x},0),t)(v,v^{\parallel})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\nabla_{v^{\parallel}}\left((\nabla_{v}u)((\vec{x},0),t)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\nabla_{v^{\parallel}}\left(\frac{a^{ni}}{a^{nn}}\partial_{i}u((\vec{x},0),t)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Thus, Equation (
3.1.21
) has also been established for
k=2
. Next, we take care of
k=3
. Let
\alpha,\beta\in\set{1,...,n-1}
. We differentiate the first order boundary condition
a^{ni}\partial_{i}u(\vec{x},0,t)=0
with respect to
x_{\alpha}
and
x_{\beta}
to obtain
a^{ni}\partial_{i\alpha\beta}u(\vec{x},0,t)=0
. Inserting into the third order boundary condition from Equation (
3.1.2
), we get

0=\sum_{i,k,l=1}^{n}a^{ni}a^{kl}\partial_{ijk}u=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[a^{ni}a^{nn}\partial_{inn}u+2\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n-1}a^{ni}a^{n\alpha}\partial_{in\alpha}u\right].

We divide by
(a^{nn})^{2}
and use that
v^{i}=\frac{a^{ni}}{a^{nn}}
as well as
v^{\parallel}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n-1}v^{\alpha}e_{\alpha}
. This shows

\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,e_{n},e_{n}]+2\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,v^{\parallel},e_{n}]=0.

(3.1.23)

Equation (
3.1.23
) and the first boundary condition
\nabla_{v}u((\vec{x},0),t)\equiv 0
imply

\displaystyle\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,v,v]

\displaystyle=\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,v^{\parallel}+e_{n},v^{\parallel}+e_{n}]

\displaystyle=\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,v^{\parallel},v^{\parallel}]+2\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,e_{n},v^{\parallel}]+\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,e_{n},e_{n}]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{v^{\parallel}}\nabla_{v^{\parallel}}\left((\nabla_{v}u)((\vec{x},0),t)\right)

\displaystyle=0.

(3.1.24)

We rewrite Equation (
3.1.23
) as

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,e_{n},e_{n}]+2\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,v^{\parallel},e_{n}]

\displaystyle=\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,e_{n},e_{n}]+\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,v^{\parallel},e_{n}]+\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,v^{\parallel},e_{n}]

\displaystyle=\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,e_{n}+v^{\parallel},e_{n}]+\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,v^{\parallel},e_{n}]

\displaystyle=\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,v,e_{n}]+\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,v^{\parallel},e_{n}].

(3.1.25)

Combining Equations (
3.1.23
), (
3.1.24
) and (
3.1.25
), we deduce

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{n}\rightarrow 0^{-}}\frac{\partial^{3}\tilde{u}}{\partial x_{n}^{3}}((\vec{x},x_{n}),t)=

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{n}\rightarrow 0^{-}}\bigg(\frac{\partial^{3}u}{\partial x_{n}^{3}}(x-2x_{n}v,t)+3\nabla^{3}u(x-2x_{n}v,t)[e_{n},e_{n},-2v]

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07182pt+3\nabla^{3}u(x-2x_{n}v,t)[e_{n},-2v,-2v]+\nabla^{3}u(x-2x_{n}v,t)[-2v,-2v,-2v]\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{3}u}{\partial x_{n}^{3}}((\vec{x},0),t)-6\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[e_{n},e_{n},v]+12\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[e_{n},v,v]

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07182pt-8\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[v,v,v]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{3}u}{\partial x_{n}^{3}}((\vec{x},0),t)-6\left(\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[e_{n},e_{n},v]-2\nabla^{3}u((\vec{x},0),t)[e_{n},v,v]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{3}u}{\partial x_{n}^{3}}((\vec{x},0),t).

So, Equation (
3.1.21
) holds for
k=3
. Finally, for
k=4
we use that in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(-\infty,0)\times I
,
\tilde{u}
solves

\partial_{nnnn}\tilde{u}(x,t)=-\frac{\dot{\tilde{u}}(x,t)}{(a^{nn})^{2}}-\frac{1}{(a^{nn})^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j,k,l=1\\
i+j+k+l\neq 4n\end{subarray}}^{n}a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}\tilde{u}(x,t).

As the right-hand side only contains derivatives with respect to
x_{n}
of order
\leq 3
, and Equation (
3.1.21
) is already proven for
k=0,1,2,3
, we deduce that it also holds for
k=4
.
∎

Lemma
3.1.8
allows us to deduce the following corollaries to Corollaries
3.1.5
,
3.1.6
and
3.1.7
. We only prove the first of the following three corollaries, as the strategy for proving them is always the same.

Corollary 3.1.9
.

Let
u\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R})
satisfy Equation (
3.1.1
) as well as
|u(x,t)|\leq C(1+d((x,t),0))^{4+\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
C>0
and all
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
. If
a^{ni}\partial_{i}u((\vec{x},0),t)
and
a^{ni}a^{kl}\partial_{ikl}u((\vec{x},0),t)
are parabolic polynomials of parabolic degrees at most 3 and 1 respectively, then
u
is a parabolic polynomial of parabolic degree
\operatorname{deg}(u)\leq 4
.

Proof.

Using the same argument as in the proof of Corollary
3.1.7
, we may assume without loss of generality that
a^{ni}\partial_{i}u((\vec{x},0),t)=a^{ni}a^{kl}\partial_{ikl}u((\vec{x},0),t)\equiv 0
. So, we can apply Lemma
3.1.8
and define a solution
\tilde{u}(x,t)\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
of Equation (
3.1.1
). For
x_{n}<0
, we estimate

|\tilde{u}(x,t)=|u(x-2x_{n}v,t)|\leq C(1+d((x-2x_{n}v,t),0))^{4+\gamma}\leq C(1+d((x,t),0))^{4+\gamma}.

We may therefore apply Corollary
3.1.6
and deduce the corollary.

∎

The following two corollaries can be established by essentially repeating the same proof.

Corollary 3.1.10
.

Let
u\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty))
satisfy Equation (
3.1.1
) and
|u(x,t)|\leq C(1+d((x,t),0))^{4+\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
C>0
and all
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)
. If
u(x,0)
is a polynomial of degree at most 4 and

a^{ni}\partial_{i}u((\vec{x},0),t)
and
a^{ni}a^{kl}\partial_{ikl}u((\vec{x},0),t)
are parabolic polynomials of parabolic degrees at most 3 and 1 respectively, then
u
is a parabolic polynomial of parabolic degree
\operatorname{deg}(u)\leq 4
.

Corollary 3.1.11
.

Let
u\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times(-\infty,0])
satisfy Equation (
3.1.1
) and
|u(x,t)|\leq C(1+d((x,t),0))^{4+\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
C>0
and all
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times(-\infty,0]
. If

a^{ni}\partial_{i}u((\vec{x},0),t)
and
a^{ni}a^{kl}\partial_{ikl}u((\vec{x},0),t)
are parabolic polynomials of parabolic degrees at most 3 and 1 respectively, then
u
is a parabolic polynomial of parabolic degree
\operatorname{deg}(u)\leq 4
.

3.2
Simon’s Blow-up Argument

Throughout this section, let
a^{ij}
denote the entries of a strictly elliptic matrix. We consider the parabolic operator

Pu:=\partial_{t}u+a^{ij}a^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}u.

(3.2.1)

Let further
\Lambda>0
and
\theta>0
such that

|a^{ij}|\leq\Lambda\textrm{ for all $1\leq i,j\leq n$}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pta^{ij}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\geq\theta|\xi|^{2}\textrm{ for all }\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(3.2.2)

Lemma 3.2.1
.

There exists a constant
C(n,\gamma,\Lambda,\theta)
such that for all constant coefficient operators
P
of the form (
3.2.1
) satisfying Assumption (
3.2.2
) and all
u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})

[D^{4,1}u]_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}}^{(0)}\leq C(n,\gamma,\Lambda,\theta)[Pu]_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}}^{(0)}.

Proof.

For the duration of the proof, we write
[\cdot]_{\gamma}:=[\cdot]_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}}^{(0)}
. The proof is by contradiction. If the lemma were false we could find operators
P_{k}
of the form (
3.2.1
) that satisfy Assumption (
3.2.2
) and functions
u_{k}
such that
[D^{4,1}u_{k}]_{\gamma}>k[P_{k}u_{k}]_{\gamma}
. We define

v_{k}:=\frac{u_{k}}{[D^{4,1}u_{k}]_{\gamma}}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{so that}\hskip 14.22636pt[D^{4,1}v_{k}]_{\gamma}=1\textrm{ and }[P_{k}v_{k}]_{\gamma}<\frac{1}{k}.

[D^{4,1}v_{k}]_{\gamma}=1
implies the existence of
p_{k}=(x_{k},t_{k})\neq q_{k}=(y_{k},s_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\epsilon_{0}(n)\in(0,1)
such that

|D^{4,1}v_{k}(p_{k})-D^{4,1}v_{k}(q_{k})|\geq\epsilon_{0}(n)d(p_{k},q_{k})^{\gamma}.

(3.2.3)

Here
d
is the parabolic metric from Definition
3.1.2
. We put
\lambda_{k}:=d(p_{k},q_{k})
and let

w_{k}(x,t):=\lambda_{k}^{-4-\gamma}v_{k}(q_{k}+(\lambda_{k}x,\lambda_{k}^{4}t)).

(3.2.4)

Note that
\dot{w}_{k}(x,t)=\lambda_{k}^{-\gamma}\dot{v}_{k}(q_{k}+(\lambda_{k}x,\lambda_{k}^{4}t))
and
\nabla_{\alpha}w_{k}(x,t)=\lambda_{k}^{-\gamma}(\nabla_{\alpha}v_{k})(q_{k}+(\lambda_{k}x,\lambda_{k}^{4}t))
for all multi indices
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
of length 4. This implies

[D^{4,1}w_{k}]_{\gamma}=[D^{4,1}v_{k}]_{\gamma}=1\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt[P_{k}w_{k}]_{\gamma}=[P_{k}v_{k}]_{\gamma}<\frac{1}{k}.

(3.2.5)

Let
\tilde{p}_{k}:=(\lambda_{k}^{-1}(x_{k}-y_{k}),\lambda_{k}^{-4}(t_{k}-s_{k}))
. In view of Estimate (
3.2.3
) and the definition of
w_{k}
we get

|D^{4,1}w_{k}(\tilde{p}_{k})-D^{4,1}w_{k}(0)|\geq\epsilon_{0}(n).

(3.2.6)

In a final step, we modify
w_{k}
by subtracting the parabolic Taylor polynomial

\tilde{w}_{k}(x,t):=w_{k}(x,t)-\dot{w}_{k}(0,0)t-\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 4}\frac{\nabla_{\alpha}w_{k}(0,0)}{\alpha!}x^{\alpha}.

This ensure that
\partial_{t}^{k}\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{w}_{k}(0,0)=0
whenever
0\leq 4k+|\alpha|\leq 4
. Note that
D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}
differs from
D^{4,1}w_{k}
only by a constant. So Estimates (
3.2.5
) and (
3.2.6
) transform into

[D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}=1,\hskip 14.22636pt[P_{k}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}<\frac{1}{k}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt|D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}(\tilde{p}_{k})|\geq\epsilon_{0}(n).

(3.2.7)

We claim that
(\tilde{p}_{k})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
is bounded and that
(\tilde{w}_{k})\subset C^{4,1,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
is locally bounded. The first claim follows from

d(\tilde{p}_{k},0)=d\left(\left(\frac{x_{k}-y_{k}}{\lambda_{k}},\frac{t_{k}-s_{k}}{\lambda_{k}^{4}},0\right)\right)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}d((x_{k}-y_{k},t_{k}-s_{k}),0)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}d(p_{k},q_{k})=1.

Next, note that
[D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}=1
and
D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}(0,0)=0
imply

|D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}(x,t)|\leq|D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}(0,0)|+C(|x|^{\gamma}+|t|^{\frac{\gamma}{4}})\leq C(|x|+|t|^{\frac{1}{4}})^{\gamma}.

(3.2.8)

Using
\dot{\tilde{w}}_{k}(0,0)=\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{w}_{k}(0,0)=0
for
0\leq|\alpha|\leq 4
and the intermediate value theorem, we deduce that for any multi-index
|\alpha|\leq 3

|\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{w}_{k}(x,t)|\leq C(n)(|x|+|t|^{\frac{1}{4}})^{4-|\alpha|+\gamma}.

(3.2.9)

Estimates (
3.2.8
) and (
3.2.9
) imply that
(w_{k})\subset C^{4,1,\gamma}(K\times[0,T])
is bounded for every compact set
K\times[0,T]\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
and hence we can pass to a subsequence along which
\tilde{p}_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{p}
and
\tilde{w}_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{w}
in
C^{4,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
. As the operators
P_{k}
satisfy Assumption (
3.2.2
), we may, after potentially passing to another subsequence, assume that
P_{k}\rightarrow P
, where
P
is an operator of the form (
3.2.1
) that satisfies Assumption (
3.2.2
). As
\dot{\tilde{w}}_{k}(0,0)=\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{w}_{k}(0,0)=0
for all
|\alpha|\leq 4
the same remains true for
\tilde{w}
. Taking
k\rightarrow\infty
in Estimate (
3.2.7
), we deduce

\displaystyle[P\tilde{w}]_{\gamma}\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}[P_{k}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{ and, using $P\tilde{w}(0)=0$, we get }P\tilde{w}=0,

(3.2.10)

\displaystyle|\tilde{w}(x,t)|=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}|\tilde{w}_{k}(x,t)|\leq C(n)(|x|+|t|^{\frac{1}{4}})^{4+\gamma}.

(3.2.11)

By Corollary
3.1.6
,
\tilde{w}
is a parabolic polynomial of degreee
\operatorname{deg}(\tilde{w})\leq 4
. In particular
D^{4,1}\tilde{w}
is a constant and therefore
D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(\tilde{p})=D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(0)=0
. However, taking
k\rightarrow\infty
in Estimate (
3.2.7
), we find

|D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(\tilde{p})|=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}|D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}(\tilde{p}_{k})|\geq\epsilon_{0}(n).

Thus we have arrived at a contradiction and the lemma follows.
∎

Following essentially the same argument, we can obtain similar estimates when we have either a temporal or a spatial boundary or both. In the following, we give the respective results and comment on the required changes in the proofs.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

There exists a constant
C(n,\gamma,\Lambda,\theta)
such that for all constant coefficient operators
P
of the form (
3.2.1
) satisfying Assumption (
3.2.2
) and all
u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[0,\infty))

[D^{4,1}u]_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[0,\infty)}^{(0)}\leq C(n,\gamma,\Lambda,\theta)\left([Pu]_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[0,\infty)}^{(0)}+[\nabla^{4}u(\cdot,0)]_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n}}\right).

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we only write
[\cdot]_{\gamma}
for the various Hölder seminorms. We follow the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
and assume the lemma was false. Then we could find operators
P_{k}
of the form (
3.2.1
) that satisfy Assumption (
3.2.2
) and functions
u_{k}
such that

[D^{4,1}u_{k}]_{\gamma}>k\left([P_{k}u_{k}]_{\gamma}+[\nabla^{4}u_{k}(\cdot,0)]_{\gamma}\right).

Next, we choose
p_{k}=(x_{k},t_{k})
,
q_{k}=(y_{k},s_{k})
as in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
. Without loss of generality
t_{k}\geq s_{k}
. We define
\tilde{p}_{k}
and the functions
\tilde{w}_{k}
as we did in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
. However, in view of Equation (
3.2.4
), as
u_{k}
are only defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[0,\infty)
, the functions
w_{k}
and
\tilde{w}_{k}
are only defined for
t\geq-\lambda_{k}^{-4}s_{k}
. Following the arguments in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle[D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}=1,\hskip 14.22636pt|\tilde{w}_{k}(x,t)|\leq C(n)(|x|+|t|^{\frac{1}{4}})^{4+\gamma},

(3.2.12)

\displaystyle|D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}(\tilde{p}_{k})-D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}(0)|\geq\epsilon_{0}(n),\hskip 14.22636pt\tilde{p}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[0,\infty),

(3.2.13)

\displaystyle[\nabla^{4}\tilde{w}_{k}(\cdot,-\lambda_{k}^{-4}s_{k})]_{\gamma}<\frac{1}{k}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt[P_{k}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}<\frac{1}{k},

(3.2.14)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{l}\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{w}_{k}(0,0)=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{whenever}\hskip 14.22636pt0\leq 4l+|\alpha|\leq 4.

(3.2.15)

After passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
\lambda_{k}^{-4}s_{k}\rightarrow\tau\in[0,\infty]
,
\tilde{p}_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{p}
,
P_{k}\rightarrow P
and
\tilde{w}_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{w}
in
C^{4,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
. Now there are two cases to consider: First, if
\tau=\infty
, the limiting function
\tilde{w}
is defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
and we can derive the same contradiction as in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
. If, however,
\tau<\infty
, we obtain a limiting function
\tilde{w}\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[-\tau,\infty))
. Letting
k\rightarrow\infty
in (
3.2.15
) yields
\partial_{t}^{l}\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{w}_{k}(0,0)=0
whenever
0\leq 4l+|\alpha|\leq 4
. Letting
k\rightarrow\infty
in Estimates (
3.2.12
), (
3.2.13
) and (
3.2.14
), we get

\displaystyle[P\tilde{w}]_{\gamma}\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}[P_{k}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{ and, using $P\tilde{w}(0)=0$, we get }P\tilde{w}=0,

\displaystyle[\nabla^{4}\tilde{w}(\cdot,-\tau)]_{\gamma}\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}[\nabla^{4}\tilde{w}_{k}(\cdot,-\lambda_{k}^{-4}s_{k})]_{\gamma}=0\hskip 7.11317pt\textrm{ and hence $\tilde{w}(\cdot,-\tau)$ is a polynomial of degree $\leq 4$,}

\displaystyle|\tilde{w}(x,t)|=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}|\tilde{w}_{k}(x,t)|\leq C(n)(|x|+|t|^{\frac{1}{4}})^{4+\gamma}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt|D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(\tilde{p})-D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(0,0)|\geq\epsilon_{0}(n).

Now we may apply Corollary
3.1.7
to deduce that
\tilde{w}
is a parabolic polynomial of degree at most 4. We can now produce the same contradiction as in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
: By Corollary
3.1.7

D^{4,1}\tilde{w}
must be a constant, which contradicts
D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(\tilde{p})\neq D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(0)
.
∎

Lemma 3.2.3
.

There exists a constant
C(n,\gamma,\Lambda,\theta)
such that for all constant coefficient operators
P
of the form (
3.2.1
) satisfying Assumption (
3.2.2
) and all
u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0])

[D^{4,1}u]^{(0)}_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]}\leq C(n,\gamma,\Lambda,\theta)[Pu]^{(0)}_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0]}.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we write
[\cdot]_{\gamma}
for the Hölder seminorms. We follow the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
and assume the lemma was false. Then there exist operators
P_{k}
of the form (
3.2.1
) that satisfy Assumption (
3.2.2
) and functions
u_{k}
such that

[D^{4,1}u_{k}]_{\gamma}>k[P_{k}u_{k}]_{\gamma}.

Next, we define
p_{k}=(x_{k},t_{k})
,
q_{k}=(y_{k},s_{k})
as we did in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
. Additionally, we assume without loss of generality that
t_{k}\leq s_{k}
. We define
\tilde{p}_{k}
and the functions
\tilde{w}_{k}
as we did in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
. In view of Equation (
3.2.4
) the functions
\tilde{w}_{k}
are only defined for
t\leq-\lambda_{k}^{-4}s_{k}
. Following the arguments in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle[D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}=1,\hskip 14.22636pt|\tilde{w}_{k}(x,t)|\leq C(n)(|x|+|t|^{\frac{1}{4}})^{4+\gamma},\hskip 14.22636pt[P_{k}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}<\frac{1}{k},

(3.2.16)

\displaystyle|D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}(\tilde{p}_{k})-D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}(0)|\geq\epsilon_{0}(n),\hskip 14.22636pt\tilde{p}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,0],

(3.2.17)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{l}\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{w}_{k}(0,0)=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{whenever}\hskip 14.22636pt0\leq 4l+|\alpha|\leq 4.

(3.2.18)

After passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
\lambda_{k}^{-4}s_{k}\rightarrow\tau\in[-\infty,0]
,
\tilde{p}_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{p}
,
P_{k}\rightarrow P
and
\tilde{w}_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{w}
in
C^{4,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
. Now there are two cases to consider: First, if
\tau=-\infty
, the limiting function
\tilde{w}
is defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
and we can derive the same contradiction as in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
. If, however,
\tau>-\infty
, we obtain a limiting function
\tilde{w}\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,-\tau])
. Letting
k\rightarrow\infty
in (
3.2.18
) yields
\partial_{t}^{l}\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{w}(0,0)=0
whenever
0\leq 4l+|\alpha|\leq 4
and letting
k\rightarrow\infty
in Estimates (
3.2.16
) and (
3.2.17
), we get

\displaystyle[P\tilde{w}]_{\gamma}\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}[P_{k}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{ and, using $P\tilde{w}(0)=0$, we get }P\tilde{w}=0,

\displaystyle|\tilde{w}(x,t)|=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}|\tilde{w}_{k}(x,t)|\leq C(n)(|x|+|t|^{\frac{1}{4}})^{4+\gamma}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt|D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(\tilde{p})-D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(0)|\geq\epsilon_{0}(n).

By Corollary
3.1.5
, we deduce that
D^{4,1}\tilde{w}
is constant, which contradicts
D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(\tilde{p})\hskip-1.42271pt\neq\hskip-1.42271ptD^{4,1}\tilde{w}(0)
.
∎

Next, we formulate similar lemmas that allow for a spatial boundary. For that sake, we introduce the constant coefficient boundary operators
induced by
P
from (
3.2.1
)

B_{1}u:=a^{ni}\partial_{i}u\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636ptB_{2}u=a^{ni}a^{kl}\partial_{ikl}u.

(3.2.19)

Lemma 3.2.4
.

There exists a constant
C(n,\gamma,\Lambda,\theta)
such that for all constant coefficient operators
P
of the form (
3.2.1
) satisfying Assumption (
3.2.2
) with induced boundary operators
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
and all
u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R})

[D^{4,1}u]_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}}^{(0)}\leq C(n,\gamma,\Lambda,\theta)\left([Pu]_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}}^{(0)}+[B_{1}u]^{(3)}_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times 0\times\mathbb{R}}+[B_{2}u]^{(1)}_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times 0\times\mathbb{R}}\right).

The proof is essentially the same as for Lemma
3.2.1
. The important new observation is that
[a^{ni}\partial_{i}u]^{(3)}_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times 0\times\mathbb{R}}
and
[a^{ni}a^{kl}\partial_{ikl}u]^{(1)}_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times 0\times\mathbb{R}}
scale just like
[Pu]_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}}^{(0)}
under the parabolic scaling
(x,t)\mapsto(\lambda x,\lambda^{4}t)
. We have shown this in the motivation directly after Definition
1.1.7
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we write
[\cdot]_{\gamma}
for the Hölder seminorms. We follow the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
and assume the lemma was false. Then there exist operators
P_{k}
of the form (
3.2.1
) that satisfy Assumption (
3.2.2
) with associated boundary operators
B_{1k}
,
B_{2k}
as in (
3.2.19
) and functions
u_{k}
such that

[D^{4,1}u_{k}]_{\gamma}>k\left([P_{k}u_{k}]_{\gamma}+[B_{1k}u_{k}]_{\gamma}+[B_{2k}u_{k}]_{\gamma}\right).

We choose
p_{k}=(\vec{x}_{k},\zeta_{k},t_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
and
q_{k}=(\vec{y}_{k},\xi_{k},s_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
as in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
. Additionally, we assume without loss of generality that
\zeta_{k}\geq\xi_{k}
. We define
\tilde{p}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
and
\tilde{w}_{k}
as in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
. As
u_{k}
are only defined for
x_{n}\geq 0
, the functions
\tilde{w}_{k}
are only defined for
x_{n}\geq-\lambda_{k}^{-1}\xi_{k}
. Following the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
, we get

\displaystyle[D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}=1,\hskip 14.22636pt|\tilde{w}_{k}(x,t)|\leq C(n)(|x|+|t|^{\frac{1}{4}})^{4+\gamma},

(3.2.20)

\displaystyle[P_{k}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}+[B_{1k}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}+[B_{2k}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}<\frac{1}{k},

(3.2.21)

\displaystyle|D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}(\tilde{p}_{k})-D^{4,1}\tilde{w}_{k}(0)|\geq\epsilon_{0}(n),\hskip 14.22636pt\tilde{p}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R},

(3.2.22)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{l}\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{w}_{k}(0,0)=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{whenever}\hskip 14.22636pt0\leq 4l+|\alpha|\leq 4.

(3.2.23)

As
k\rightarrow\infty
we have
\lambda_{k}^{-1}\xi_{k}\rightarrow z\in[0,\infty]
,
\tilde{p}_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{p}
,
P_{k}\rightarrow P
and
\tilde{w}_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{w}
in
C^{4,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
. Additionally, by Assumption (
3.2.2
) and Equation (
3.2.19
), we deduce that
B_{1k}\rightarrow B_{1}
and
B_{2k}\rightarrow B_{2}
, where
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are the boundary operators induced by
P
. If
z=\infty
, the limiting function
\tilde{w}
is defined on all of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
and we deduce the same contradiction as in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
. If
z<\infty
, we obtain a limiting function
\tilde{w}\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[-z,\infty)\times\mathbb{R})
. Letting
k\rightarrow\infty
in Estimates (
3.2.20
), (
3.2.21
) and (
3.2.22
), and using (
3.2.23
), we deduce that
\tilde{w}
satisfies

\displaystyle[P\tilde{w}]_{\gamma}\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}[P_{k}\tilde{w}_{k}]_{\gamma}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{ and hence }P\tilde{w}=0,

\displaystyle[B_{1}\tilde{w}(\cdot,-z,\cdot)]_{\gamma}+[B_{2}\tilde{w}(\cdot,-z,\cdot)]_{\gamma}\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left([B_{1}\tilde{w}_{k}(\cdot,-\lambda_{k}^{-1}\xi_{k},\cdot)]_{\gamma}+[B_{2}\tilde{w}_{k}(\cdot,-\lambda_{k}^{-1}\xi_{k},\cdot)]_{\gamma}\right)=0,

\displaystyle|\tilde{w}(x,t)|=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}|\tilde{w}_{k}(x,t)|\leq C(n)(|x|+|t|^{\frac{1}{4}})^{4+\gamma}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt|D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(\tilde{p})-D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(0)|\geq\epsilon_{0}(n).

We claim that
B_{1}w
and
B_{2}w
are parabolic polynomials of degree 3 or less and 1 or less, respectively. Once this is shown, we can use Corollary
3.1.9
to deduce that
\tilde{w}
is a parabolic polynomial of degree at most 4. This produces the usual contradiction of
D^{4,1}\tilde{w}
being constant while simultaneously
|D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(\tilde{p})-D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(0)|\geq\epsilon_{0}(n)
.

We prove that
B_{1}\tilde{w}
is a parabolic polynomial of degree 3 or less. Proving that
B_{2}\tilde{w}
is a parabolic polynomial of degree 1 or less is then achieved by a similar argument. By definition of the
[\cdot]_{\gamma}^{(3)}
-seminorm
[\nabla^{3}B_{1}\tilde{w}]_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(-z)\times\mathbb{R}}^{\operatorname{space}}=0
. So
\nabla^{3}B_{1}w(\vec{x},-z,0)
is constant and hence
B_{1}w(\vec{x},-z,0)
a polynomial in
\vec{x}
of degree 3 or less. Also by definition of the
[\cdot]_{\gamma}^{(3)}
-seminorm, we deduce that
[B_{1}\tilde{w}]^{\operatorname{time}}_{\frac{3+\gamma}{4},\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(-z)\times\mathbb{R}}=0
and thus
B_{1}\tilde{w}(\vec{x},-z,t)=B_{1}\tilde{w}(\vec{x},-z,0)
. Combining these two observations, we deduce that
B_{1}\tilde{w}(\vec{x},-z,t)
is a parabolic polynomial in
(\vec{x},t)
of degree 3 or less.
∎

Finally, we consider the cases where we have a spatial and a temporal boundary.

Lemma 3.2.5
.

There exists a constant
C(n,\gamma,\Lambda,\theta)
such that for all constant coefficient operators
P
of the form (
3.2.1
) satisfying Assumption (
3.2.2
) with induced boundary operators
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
and all
u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty))

\displaystyle[D^{4,1}u]^{(0)}_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)}\leq C(n,\gamma,\Lambda,\theta)\big(

\displaystyle[Pu]_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)}^{(0)}+[B_{1}u]^{(3)}_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times 0\times[0,\infty)}

\displaystyle+[B_{2}u]^{(1)}_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times 0\times[0,\infty)}+[\nabla^{4}u(\cdot,0)]_{\gamma,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)}\big).

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we write
[\cdot]_{\gamma}
for the Hölder seminorms. We follow the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
and assume the lemma was false. Then there exist operators
P_{k}
of the form (
3.2.1
) that satisfy Assumption (
3.2.2
) with associated boundary operators
B_{1k}
,
B_{2k}
as in (
3.2.19
) and functions
u_{k}
such that

[D^{4,1}u_{k}]_{\gamma}>k\left([P_{k}u_{k}]_{\gamma}+[B_{1k}u_{k}]_{\gamma}+[B_{2k}u_{k}]_{\gamma}+[\nabla^{4}u_{k}(\cdot,0)]_{\gamma}\right).

Next, we define
p_{k}=(\vec{x}_{k},\zeta_{k},t_{k})
,
q_{k}=(\vec{y}_{k},\xi_{k},s_{k})
as we did in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\zeta_{k}\geq\xi_{k}
. We define
\tilde{p}_{k}
and the functions
\tilde{w}_{k}
as we did in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
. In view of Equation (
3.2.4
) the functions
\tilde{w}_{k}
are only defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[-\lambda_{k}^{-1}\xi_{k},\infty)\times[-\lambda_{k}^{-4}s_{k},\infty)
. After passing to some subsequence we may assume that
\lambda_{k}^{-1}\xi_{k}\rightarrow z\in[0,\infty]
,
\lambda_{k}^{-4}s_{k}\rightarrow\tau\in[0,\infty]
,
\tilde{p}_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{p}
,
P_{k}\rightarrow P
,
B_{1k}\rightarrow B_{1}
,
B_{2k}\rightarrow B_{2}
and
\tilde{w}_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{w}
in
C^{4,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
. We have the following cases:

(1)

If
\tau=z=\infty
, we obtain a limit function
\tilde{w}\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
and arrive at the same contradiction as in the proof of Lemma
3.2.1
.

(2)

If
z=\infty
but
\tau<\infty
, we obtain a limit function
\tilde{w}\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[-\tau,\infty))
and arrive at the same contradiction as in the proof of Lemma
3.2.2
.

(3)

If
\tau=\infty
but
z<\infty
, we obtain a limit function
\tilde{w}\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[-z,\infty)\times\mathbb{R})
and arrive at the same contradiction as in the proof of Lemma
3.2.4
.

(4)

If
z,\tau<\infty
, we obtain a limit function
\tilde{w}\in C^{4,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[-z,\infty)\times[-\tau,\infty))
. Following the arguments in the proofs of Lemmas
3.2.1
,
3.2.2
and
3.2.4
, the limit
\tilde{w}
satisfies:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&P\tilde{w}=0&\textrm{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[-z,\infty)\times[-\tau,\infty),\\
&B_{1}\tilde{w}(\vec{x},-z,t)&\textrm{ is a parabolic polynomial in $(\vec{x},t)$ of degree 3 or less,}\\
&B_{2}\tilde{w}(\vec{x},-z,t)&\textrm{ is a parabolic polynomial in $(\vec{x},t)$ of degree 1 or less,}\\
&\tilde{w}(x,-\tau)&\textrm{ is a polynomial of degree 4 or less,}\end{array}

\textrm{as well as}\hskip 14.22636pt|D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(\tilde{p})-D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(0)|\geq\frac{1}{2}.\hskip 119.50148pt

Using Corollary
3.1.10
,
\tilde{w}
is a parabolic polynomial of degree 4 or less and hence
D^{4,1}\tilde{w}
is constant, which contradicts
D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(0)\neq D^{4,1}\tilde{w}(\tilde{p})
.

∎

Using the same arguments but replacing
Lemma
3.2.2

with Lemma
3.2.3
