\displaystyle C\bigg(\|Pu\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M\times[0,T])}+\|u(\cdot,0)\|_{C^{4,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}+\|B_{1}u\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,T])}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\|B_{2}u\|_{C^{1,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,T])}+\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}\bigg).

Here
C
depends on
M
,
T_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\Lambda:=\|P\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M\times[0,T])}+\|B_{1}\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,T])}+\|B_{2}\|_{C^{1,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,T])}
and
\theta:=\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}(P)
but not on
T
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}=\set{(\varphi_{\nu},U_{\nu})\ }{\ 1\leq\nu\leq m}
. By definition, there exist open sets
\tilde{U}_{\nu}
such that
U_{\nu}\subset\subset\tilde{U}_{\nu}
and such that
\varphi_{\nu}
has a smooth extension onto
\tilde{U}_{\nu}
. For each
1\leq\nu\leq m
, we choose an open set
U_{\nu}^{\prime}
such that
U_{\nu}\subset\subset U_{\nu}^{\prime}\subset\subset\tilde{U}_{\nu}
. Note that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:=\set{(\varphi_{\nu},U_{\nu}^{\prime})\ }{\ 1\leq\nu\leq m}
is a good atlas on
M
. By Estimate (
4.1.2
) and the analog estimates, the operator
P
and the boundary operator
B
have norms bonded by
C\Lambda
and an ellipticity constant bounded from below by
C^{-1}\theta
with respect to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Here
C
depends only on
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\gamma
. We put
V_{\nu}:=\varphi_{\nu}(U_{\nu})
and
V_{\nu}^{\prime}:=\varphi_{\nu}(U_{\nu}^{\prime})
.

Let
u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
. For
1\leq\nu\leq m
we put
v_{\nu}:=u\circ\varphi_{\nu}^{-1}
and denote the local representation of
P
by
P_{\nu}
. If
U_{\nu}
is a boundary chart, we denote the local representation of the boundary operators by
(B_{1\nu},B_{2\nu})
.

Depending on whether
U_{\nu}
is an interior or a boundary chart, we use Corollary
3.4.4
or
3.4.5
respectively to deduce that with a constant
C=C(V_{\nu},V_{\nu}^{\prime},T_{0},\Lambda,\theta,\gamma)

\displaystyle\|v_{\nu}\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}(V_{\nu}\times[0,T])}\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\|P_{\nu}v_{\nu}\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}(V_{\nu}^{\prime}\times[0,T])}+\|v_{\nu}(\cdot,0)\|_{C^{4,\gamma}(V_{\nu}^{\prime})}+\|v_{\nu}\|_{L^{\infty}(V_{\nu}^{\prime}\times[0,T])}\right),

\displaystyle\|v_{\nu}\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}(V_{\nu}\times[0,T])}\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\|P_{\nu}v_{\nu}\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}(V_{\nu}^{\prime}\times[0,T])}+\|v_{\nu}(\cdot,0)\|_{C^{4,\gamma}(V_{\nu}^{\prime})}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\|B_{1\nu}v_{\nu}\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial_{S}V_{\nu}^{\prime}\times[0,T])}+\|B_{2\nu}v_{\nu}\|_{C^{1,0,\gamma}(\partial_{S}V_{\nu}^{\prime}\times[0,T])}+\|v_{\nu}\|_{L^{\infty}(V_{\nu}^{\prime}\times[0,T])}\right).

Taking the maximum over all
1\leq\nu\leq m
, we deduce with
C=C(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime},T_{0},\Lambda,\theta,\gamma)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M\times[0,T])}\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\|Pu\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(M\times[0,T])}+\|u(\cdot,0)\|_{C^{4,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(M)}+\|B_{1}u\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\partial M\times[0,T])}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\|B_{2}u\|_{C^{1,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\partial M\times[0,T])}+\|u\|_{L_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}^{\infty}(M\times[0,T])}\right).

As
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
are both good atlases, we can use Lemmas
1.3.3
and
1.3.5
to deduce that with
C=C(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime},T_{0},\Lambda,\theta,\gamma)

independent of
T

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M\times[0,T])}\leq C

\displaystyle\left(\|Pu\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M\times[0,T])}+\|u(\cdot,0)\|_{C^{4,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}+\|B_{1}u\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,T])}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\|B_{2}u\|_{C^{1,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,T])}+\|u\|_{L_{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}(M\times[0,T])}\right).

(4.1.5)

As
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
depends only on
\mathcal{A}
, we can drop the
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
dependence in the constant. Finally, we note that
C_{\mathcal{A}}^{4,\gamma}(M)
embeds compactly into
L_{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}(M)
and
L_{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}(M)
embeds continuously into
L_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}(M)
. So, by Ehrling’s lemma, we know that for any small
\epsilon>0

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{\mathcal{A}}(M\times[0,T])}=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{\mathcal{A}}(M\times t)}\right)\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\epsilon\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{4,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}+C(\epsilon)\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}\right).

Inserting into Estimate (
4.1.5
) and choosing
\epsilon
small enough we can absorb
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{4,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}
to the left side and the theorem follows.
∎

4.2
Canonical Boundary Conditions

Throughout this section, we assume that
L
is a
2
-elliptic operator with smooth coefficients and that
B
defines
L
-compatible boundary conditions with smooth coefficients. Considering Theorem
4.1.6
, our aim is to derive an a priori estimate of the form

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}\leq

\displaystyle C\bigg(\|\dot{u}+Lu\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M\times[0,T])}+\|u(\cdot,0)\|_{C^{4,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}+\|B_{1}u\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,T])}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\|B_{2}u\|_{C^{1,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,T])}\bigg).

To do this, we will specialize to
canonical boundary conditions
. To derive these, we collect some well-known facts from Riemannian geometry. For a thorough introduction, we refer to Lee’s book
[
13
]
(see Chapter 4 and Appendix B).

Remark 4.2.1

(Tensor Fields)
.

Let
(N,s)
be a Riemannian manifold.

(1)

A smooth
(p,q)
tensor field is a
C^{\infty}(N)
-multilinear map

T:\underbrace{T^{*}N\times...\times T^{*}N}_{\textrm{$p$ copies}}\times\underbrace{TN\times...\times TN}_{\textrm{$q$ copies}}\rightarrow C^{\infty}(N).

Such a tensor field
T
has the local representation

T=T^{i_{1},...,i_{p}}_{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ k_{1}...k_{q}}\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i_{1}}}\otimes...\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i_{p}}}\otimes dx^{k_{1}}\otimes...\otimes dx^{k_{q}}.

(2)

Let
T
be a smooth
(p,q)
tensor field on
N
and let
\nabla
denote the Levi-Cevita connection induced by
s
. Then
\nabla T
is a smooth
(p,q+1)
tensor field defined by

(\nabla T)(\omega^{1},...,\omega^{p},X_{1},...,X_{q},Y)=(\nabla_{Y}T)(\omega^{1},...,\omega^{p},X_{1},...,X_{q}).

The divergence
\nabla^{*}T
of
T
is a smooth
(p,q-1)
tensor field defined by

\nabla^{*}T(\omega^{1},...,\omega^{p},X_{1},...,X_{q-1}):=s^{ij}\nabla_{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}}T(\omega^{1},...,\omega^{p},X_{1},...,X_{q-1},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}).

Here
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
is the basis induced by an arbitrary chart and
s^{ij}
is the inverse metric tensor expressed in the same chart.

(3)

If
T
and
S
are smooth tensor fields of types
(p,0)
and
(0,p)
respectively, we define their
pairing
as
\langle T,S\rangle\in C^{\infty}(N)
by the local formula

\langle T,S\rangle=T^{i_{1}...i_{p}}S_{i_{1}...i_{p}}.

After this preparation, we present the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2.2
.

There exists a smooth metric
g
on
M
, a smooth
(3,0)
-tensor field
J
on
M
and a second order linear elliptic operator
\mathcal{L}
with smooth coefficients such that

Lu=\Delta_{g}^{2}u+\langle J,\nabla^{3}u\rangle+\mathcal{L}u.

Proof.

Let
\varphi:U\rightarrow V
and
\tilde{\varphi}:\tilde{U}\rightarrow\tilde{V}
be good charts such that
U\cap\tilde{U}\neq\emptyset
. For
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
, we put
v:=u\circ\varphi^{-1}
,
\tilde{v}:=u\circ\tilde{\varphi}^{-1}
and
\chi:=\tilde{\varphi}\circ\varphi^{-1}:\varphi(U\cap\tilde{U})\rightarrow\tilde{\varphi}(U\cap\tilde{U})
. Denoting derivatives of
v
and
\tilde{v}
of order
\leq 3
by ‘lower order terms’, we compute

\displaystyle a_{\varphi}^{ij}a_{\varphi}^{kl}\partial_{ijkl}v+\textrm{`lower order terms'}=

\displaystyle(Lu)\circ\varphi^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(Lu)\circ(\tilde{\varphi}^{-1}\circ\chi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(a^{ij}_{\tilde{\varphi}}a^{kl}_{\tilde{\varphi}}\partial_{ijkl}\tilde{v}+\textrm{`lower order terms'}\right)\circ\chi

Using
v=\tilde{v}\circ\chi
, we deduce

a^{ij}_{\tilde{\varphi}}a^{kl}_{\tilde{\varphi}}\partial_{ijkl}\tilde{v}=\partial_{i}\chi^{a}\partial_{j}\chi^{b}\partial_{k}\chi^{c}\partial_{l}\chi^{d}a^{ij}_{\varphi}a^{kl}_{\varphi}\partial_{abcd}\tilde{v}+\textrm{`lower order terms'}.

As
\tilde{v}
is arbitrary we deduce that
a_{\tilde{\varphi}}^{ij}=\partial_{a}\chi^{i}\partial_{b}\chi^{j}a_{\varphi}^{ab}
and hence we can define a smooth
(2,0)
-tensor field in local coordinates by
g^{ij}:=a^{ij}_{\varphi}
. As
a^{ij}_{\varphi}
is an elliptic matrix, we can define a Riemannian metric using the inverse
g_{ij}
. Since in local coordinates
\Delta_{g}=g^{ij}\partial_{ij}+\textrm{`lower order terms'}
, we deduce that
L=\Delta_{g}^{2}+\textrm{`lower order terms'}
. We consider the operator
T:=L-\Delta_{g}^{2}
. This is a third-order operator and so locally
T=J^{ijk}\partial_{ijk}+\textrm{`lower order terms'}
. Using the formula
T=L-\Delta_{g}^{2}
, we also see that
T
is a geometric object.
Using the same arguments we have used before, we can use
J^{ijk}
to define a smooth
(3,0)
-tensor field
J
.
Using that in local coordinates

(\nabla_{g}^{3}u)_{ijk}=\partial_{ijk}u+\textrm{`lower order terms'}
, we deduce

Tu=J^{ijk}(\nabla^{3}u)_{ijk}+\textrm{lower order terms}=\langle J,\nabla^{3}u\rangle+\textrm{lower order terms}.

As
T
is a geometric operator and
\langle J,\nabla^{3}u\rangle
is geometric, we deduce that
\mathcal{L}u:=Tu-\langle J,\nabla^{3}u\rangle
defines a geometric second order operator and so the lemma follows.
∎

Using Lemma
4.2.2
, we can now formulate
canonical boundary conditions
for
L
.

Definition 4.2.3

(Canonical Boundary Conditions)
.

Let
g
and
J
be as in Lemma
4.2.2
and denote by
\nu
the exterior unit normal along
\partial M
with respect to
g
. The boundary conditions

\left\{\begin{aligned} B_{1}(u):=&\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu},\\
B_{2}(u):=&\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}+\langle J,\nabla^{2}u\otimes\nu^{\flat}\rangle,\end{aligned}\right.

are called
canonical boundary conditions associated to
L
. Here
\nu^{\flat}:=g(\nu,\cdot)\in T^{*}\partial M
.

To justify the name canonical, we recall the following fact from Riemannian geometry. For a guided proof, we refer to an exercise in Lee’s book
[
13
]
(see Chapter 5, Problem 5-16).

Proposition 4.2.4
.

Let
(N,s)
be a Riemannian manifold,
\nu
denote the exterior unit normal along
\partial M
and let
T
and
S
be smooth tensor fields of types
(p,0)
and
(0,p-1)
respectively. Then

\int_{N}\langle T,\nabla S\rangle d\mu_{s}=-\int_{N}\langle\nabla^{*}T,S\rangle d\mu_{s}+\int_{\partial N}\langle T,S\otimes\nu^{\flat}\rangle dS_{s}.

Here
\nu^{\flat}:=s(\nu,\cdot)\in T^{*}\partial N
.

Lemma 4.2.5
.

The canonical boundary conditions
B=(B_{1},B_{2})
are
L
-compatible. Additionally, putting

\beta[u,v]:=\int_{M}\Delta_{g}v\Delta_{g}ud\mu_{g}-\int_{M}\langle\nabla_{g}^{*}(vJ),\nabla^{2}u\rangle d\mu_{g}+\int_{M}v\mathcal{L}ud\mu_{g},

(4.2.1)

for
u,v\in C^{4}(M)
, we have the formula

\int_{M}vLud\mu_{g}=\beta[u,v]+\int_{\partial M}B_{2}(u)v-\Delta_{g}vB_{1}(u)dS_{g}.

Proof.

The fact that the canonical boundary conditions are
L
-compatible follows from the local formulas in Equation (
4.1.3
). To prove the formula, we use Lemma
4.2.2
and compute

\displaystyle\int_{M}vLud\mu_{g}=

\displaystyle\int_{M}v\Delta_{g}^{2}ud\mu_{g}+\int_{M}\langle vJ,\nabla^{3}u\rangle d\mu_{g}+\int_{M}v\mathcal{L}ud\mu_{g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\Delta_{g}v\Delta_{g}ud\mu_{g}-\int_{M}\langle\nabla_{g}^{*}(vJ),\nabla^{2}u\rangle d\mu_{g}+\int_{M}v\mathcal{L}ud\mu_{g}

\displaystyle+\int_{\partial M}\left(v\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}-\Delta_{g}v\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}\right)dS_{g}+\int_{\partial M}\langle vJ,\nabla^{2}u\otimes\nu^{\flat}\rangle dS_{g}.

∎

Next, we establish an estimate for
\beta[u,u]
that we will use as a form of a coercivity estimate. To do this, we require the following definition.

Definition 4.2.6
.

Let
T
be a
(p,q)
tensor field on
M
of class
C^{k}
and
\mathcal{A}=\set{(\varphi_{\nu},U_{\nu})\ }{\ 1\leq\nu\leq m}
be a good atlas on
M
. Let
T^{i_{1}...i_{p}}_{\varphi_{\nu},\ \ \ j_{1}...j_{q}}
be the coordinates of
T
in the chart
\varphi_{\nu}
. We define

\|T\|_{C^{k}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}:=\max_{1\leq\nu\leq m}\max_{1\leq i_{1},...,i_{p}\leq n}\max_{1\leq j_{1},...,j_{q}\leq n}\|T^{i_{1}...i_{p}}_{\varphi_{\nu},\ \ \ j_{1}...j_{q}}\|_{C^{k}(V_{\nu})}.

Remark 4.2.7

(Conventions for the Rest of this Section)
.

For the rest of this section, we fix a good atlas
\mathcal{A}
, denote by
B=(B_{1},B_{2})
the canonical boundary conditions associated to
L
and by
J
the
(3,0)
tensor field provided by Lemma
4.2.2
. We define

\displaystyle\Lambda:=\|L\|_{C^{0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}+\|B_{1}\|_{C^{3,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}+\|B_{2}\|_{C^{1,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}+\|J\|_{C^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}.

Additionally, we denote the metric from Lemma
4.2.2
by
g
. Note that
\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}(L)
depends only on
g
.

Lemma 4.2.8

(Approximate Coercivity)
.

There exist
C=C(M,\gamma,g,\Lambda)>0
and
\kappa=\kappa(M,\gamma,g,\Lambda)>0
such that for all
u\in C^{4}(M)

\beta[u,u]\geq\kappa\|u\|_{W^{2,2}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}^{2}-C\left(\|u\|_{L^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}^{2}+\|B_{1}u\|_{L^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M)}^{2}+\|u\|_{C^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}\|B_{1}u\|_{C^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M)}\right).

Proof.

Throughout the proof, let
C
denote a constant that is allowed to depend only on
M
,
\gamma
,
g
and
\Lambda
. Throughout the proof, we will drop the subscript
\mathcal{A}
on all norms. Additionally, we will use the equivalence of the norms to use the norms
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}
and
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{g}(M)}
interchangeably. Let
u\in C^{4}(M)
and
h:=\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}
. Noting that
\mathcal{L}
is a second-order operator, we estimate

\left|\int_{M}\langle\nabla_{g}^{*}(uJ),\nabla^{2}u\rangle-u\mathcal{L}ud\mu_{g}\right|\leq C\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(M)}\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}

and deduce

\beta[u,u]\geq\int_{M}(\Delta_{g}u)^{2}d\mu_{g}-C\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(M)}\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}.

(4.2.2)

We now derive suitable estimates for
\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(M)}
and
\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}
. Using Lemma
1.4.9
, we estimate

\displaystyle\int_{M}|\nabla u|^{2}d\mu_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial M}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}udS_{g}-\int_{M}u\Delta_{g}ud\mu_{g}

\displaystyle\leq\|h\|_{L^{2}(\partial M)}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\partial M)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}\|\Delta_{g}u\|_{L^{2}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|h\|_{L^{2}(\partial M)}^{2}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}\|\Delta_{g}u\|_{L^{2}(M)}\right)+C(\epsilon)\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}+\epsilon\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}.

Absorbing
\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}
to the left, we deduce

\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(M)}^{2}\leq C\left(\|h\|_{L^{2}(\partial M)}^{2}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}\|\Delta_{g}u\|_{L^{2}(M)}\right).

(4.2.3)

Next, we derive an estimate for
\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}^{2}
. To do this, we use Bochner’s identity
1
1

1

For a guided proof, we refer to Lee’s book
[
13
]
(see Chapter 7, Problem 7-7)

\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{g}|\nabla u|^{2}=g(\nabla\Delta_{g}u,\nabla u)+|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}+\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nabla u,\nabla u).

Integrating over
M
and recalling
h:=\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}
, we get

\int_{M}(\Delta_{g}u)^{2}d\mu_{g}=\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}+\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nabla u,\nabla u)d\mu_{g}+\int_{\partial M}\Delta_{g}uh-g(\nabla_{\nu}\nabla u,\nabla u)dS_{g}.

(4.2.4)

In local coordinates
\nabla^{2}u=\partial_{ij}u-\Gamma^{k}_{\ ij}\partial_{k}u
and
\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nabla u,\nabla u)=\operatorname{Ric}_{g}^{ij}\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}u
. Hence, there exists (a perhaps small) constant
c_{0}>0
and a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}d\mu_{g}\geq c_{0}\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}^{2}-C\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(M)}^{2},

(4.2.5)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{M}\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nabla u,\nabla u)d\mu_{g}\right|\leq C\int_{M}|\nabla u|^{2}d\mu_{g}\leq C\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(M)}^{2},

(4.2.6)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\partial M}\Delta_{g}uhdS_{g}\right|\leq C\|u\|_{C^{2}(M)}\|h\|_{L^{2}(\partial M)}.

(4.2.7)

Next, we estimate the remaining boundary integral in Equation (
4.2.4
). Let
p\in\partial M
and denote the
g_{p}
-orthogonal projection from
T_{p}M
onto
T_{p}\partial M
by
\pi_{T_{p}\partial M}
. Using
\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}=h
, we write

\displaystyle\nabla u(p)=

\displaystyle\nabla u(p)-g_{p}(\nabla u(p),\nu(p))\nu(p)+g(\nabla u(p),\nu(p))\nu(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{T_{p}\partial M}(\nabla u(p))+\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}\bigg|_{p}\nu(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla^{\partial M}u(p)+h(p)\nu(p).

(4.2.8)

We recall the formulas
\nabla^{2}u[X,Y]=g(\nabla_{X}\nabla u,Y)=\nabla_{X}(\nabla_{Y}u)-du(\nabla_{X}Y)
. Since
\nabla^{\partial M}u\in T\partial M
is a tangential vector field along
\partial M
, we can use Equation (
4.2.8
) to compute

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{\nu}\nabla u,\nabla u)=

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{\nu}\nabla u,\nabla^{\partial M}u)+hg(\nabla_{\nu}\nabla u,\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}u[\nu,\nabla^{\partial M}u]+h\nabla^{2}u[\nu,\nu]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{\nabla^{\partial M}u}\nabla_{\nu}u-du(\nabla_{\nabla^{\partial M}u}\nu)+h\nabla^{2}u[\nu,\nu]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{\nabla^{\partial M}u}h-du(\nabla_{\nabla^{\partial M}u}\nu)+h\nabla^{2}u[\nu,\nu].

The first and third terms contain products of derivatives of
u
and
h
or orders at most 2 and 1, respectively. The second term is quadratic in
\nabla u
. Hence

\displaystyle\int_{\partial M}|g(\nabla_{\nu}\nabla u,\nabla u)|dS_{g}\leq

\displaystyle C\|h\|_{C^{1}(\partial M)}\|u\|_{C^{2}(M)}+C\int_{\partial M}|\nabla u|^{2}dS_{g}.

(4.2.9)

Inserting Estimates (
4.2.5
), (
4.2.6
), (
4.2.7
) and (
4.2.9
) into Estimate (
4.2.4
) and subsequently inserting the trace estimate from Lemma
1.4.9
, we deduce that for all small
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle c_{0}\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{g}u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}+C\left(\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(M)}^{2}+\|u\|_{C^{2}(M)}\|h\|_{C^{1}(\partial M)}+\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\partial M)}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{g}u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}+C\|u\|_{C^{2}(M)}\|h\|_{C^{1}(\partial M)}+C(\epsilon)\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(M)}^{2}+\epsilon\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}^{2}.

For small enough
\epsilon
, we deduce that with a (perhaps small) constant
c_{1}>0

\|\Delta_{g}u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}\geq c_{1}\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}^{2}-C\left(\|u\|_{C^{2}(M)}\|h\|_{C^{1}(\partial M)}+\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(M)}^{2}\right).

(4.2.10)

Let
\delta>0
be a small parameter. Using Estimates (
4.2.2
) and (
4.2.10
) and choosing
\delta>0
small enough, we estimate

\displaystyle\beta[u,u]\geq

\displaystyle\int_{M}(\Delta_{g}u)^{2}d\mu_{g}-C(\delta)\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(M)}^{2}-\delta\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle c_{1}\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}^{2}-C\|u\|_{C^{2}(M)}\|h\|_{C^{1}(\partial M)}-C(\delta)\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(M)}^{2}-\delta\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{2c_{1}}{3}\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}^{2}-C\|u\|_{C^{2}(M)}\|h\|_{C^{1}(\partial M)}-C\|u\|_{W^{1,2}(M)}^{2}.

Let
\mu>0
be another small parameter. Inserting Estimate (
4.2.3
), using
\|\Delta_{g}u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}\leq C\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}^{2}
and choosing
\mu
small enough, we deduce

\displaystyle\beta[u,u]\geq

\displaystyle\frac{2c_{1}}{3}\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}^{2}-C\left(\|u\|_{C^{2}(M)}\|h\|_{C^{1}(\partial M)}+\|h\|_{L^{2}(\partial M)}^{2}\right)-C(\mu)\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}-\mu\|\Delta_{g}u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{c_{1}}{2}\|u\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}^{2}-C\left(\|u\|_{C^{2}(M)}\|h\|_{C^{1}(\partial M)}+\|h\|_{L^{2}(\partial M)}^{2}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}\right).

∎

Using the approximate coercivity of
\beta
, we can now eliminate the
L^{2}
-norm of
u
in Theorem
4.1.6
.

Corollary 4.2.9
.

Let
T>0
,
L
be a 2-elliptic operator with smooth coefficients and
P:=\partial_{t}+L
. Let further
B=(B_{1},B_{2})
denote the canonical boundary conditions associated to
L
. Using the conventions from Remark
4.2.7
, there exists a constant
C=C(M,T,\gamma,g,\Lambda)
such that for all
u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M\times[0,T])}\leq

\displaystyle C\bigg(\|Pu\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M\times[0,T])}+\|u(\cdot,0)\|_{C^{4,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}+\|B_{1}u\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,T])}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\|B_{2}u\|_{C^{1,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,T])}\bigg).

By eliminating the
L^{2}
-norm from Theorem
4.1.6
, the constant
C
in Corollary
4.2.9
does generally depend on
T
and not only on a lower bound
T_{0}
. Also note, that
C
depends on
\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}(L)
via
g
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we will only make the dependence on
T
explicit in the notation. Also we will use the equivalence of the norms
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}
and
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{g}(M)}
to use them interchangeably. Finally, we will drop the subscripts
\mathcal{A}
or
g
on all norms.

We define
f:=\dot{u}+Lu
,
h_{1}:=B_{1}u
and
h_{2}:=B_{2}u
. Using Lemmas
4.2.5
and
4.2.8
, we estimate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{M}u(t)^{2}d\mu_{g}=\int_{M}u(t)(f(t)-Lu(t))d\mu_{g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}f(t)u(t)d\mu_{g}-\beta[u(t),u(t)]-\int_{\partial M}h_{2}(t)u(t)-h_{1}(t)\Delta_{g}u(t)dS_{g}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial M}u(t)h_{2}(t)-h_{1}(t)\Delta_{g}u(t)dS_{g}-\kappa\|u(t)\|_{W^{2,2}(M)}^{2}

\displaystyle+C\bigg(\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}+\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}+\|h_{1}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\partial M)}^{2}+\|u(t)\|_{C^{2}(M)}\|h_{1}(t)\|_{C^{1}(\partial M)}\bigg).

Let
\epsilon>0
be a small parameter and
Q_{0}:=\|f\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}+\|h_{1}\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])}+\|h_{2}\|_{C^{1,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])}
. Using Young’s inequality we get that for some constant
C_{1}
independent of
\epsilon
and another constant
C(\epsilon)

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}\leq C_{1}\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}+C(\epsilon)Q_{0}^{2}+\epsilon\|u\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}^{2}.

Multiplying with the integrating factor
e^{-2C_{1}t}\leq 1
, we deduce

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left[e^{-2C_{1}t}\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}\right]\leq

\displaystyle 2\left(C(\epsilon)Q_{0}^{2}+\epsilon\|u\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}^{2}\right).

Integrating this inequality, we obtain the estimate

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}\leq e^{2C_{1}T}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}+Te^{2C_{1}T}C(\epsilon)Q_{0}^{2}+\epsilon Te^{2C_{1}T}\|u\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}^{2}.

Inserting into the estimate from Theorem
4.1.6
, we get

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}\leq

\displaystyle C(T,\epsilon)\bigg(\|Pu\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}+\|u(\cdot,0)\|_{C^{4,\gamma}(M)}+\|B_{1}u\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\|B_{2}u\|_{C^{1,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])}\bigg)+\epsilon C(T)\|u\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}.

Choosing
\epsilon=\epsilon(T)
small enough, we can absorb
\|u\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}}
to the left-hand side and deduce the corollary.
∎

4.3
Existence of Solutions

Throughout this section, we assume that
g
is a smooth Riemannian metric on
M
. We denote the Laplace-Beltrami operator by
\Delta_{g}
and the exterior unit normal along
\partial M
by
\nu
.

4.3.1
The Galerkin Approximation

We wish to construct solutions by expanding into Neumann eigenfunctions. To deduce convergence of the resulting sequences, we require the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem. This result is well-known in the literature. As an example, we point to
[
10
]
, Chapter 1, Section 3, Theorem 3.10.

Theorem 4.3.1

(Arzelà-Ascoli Theorem)
.

Let
(A,d)
be a compact metric space and
(B,d^{\prime})
be a metric space. Let
f_{k}:A\rightarrow B
be a sequence of maps and assume the following:

(1)

f_{k}
is uniformly bounded. That is there exists
b_{0}\in B
and
R>0
such that
f_{k}(A)\subset B_{R}^{d^{\prime}}(b_{0})
for all
k
.

(2)

f_{k}
is equicontinuous. That is: For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
such that whenever
d(a,\tilde{a})<\delta
we have
d^{\prime}(f_{k}(a),f_{k}(\tilde{a}))<\epsilon
for all
k
.

(3)

For each
a\in A
the sequence
f_{k}(a)
has a convergent subsequence.

Then there exists
f\in C^{0}(A,B)
such that along a subsequence
f_{k}\rightarrow f
in
C^{0}(A,B)
as
k\rightarrow\infty
.

We require the following corollary:

Corollary 4.3.2
.

Let
X
and
Y
be Banach spaces such that
X\hookrightarrow\hookrightarrow Y
compactly,
T>0
and
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then
C^{m+1}([0,T],X)\hookrightarrow\hookrightarrow C^{m}([0,T],Y)
compactly.

Proof.

We first consider the case
m=0
. Let
f_{k}\subset C^{1}([0,T],X)
be bounded. We prove that the sequence
(f_{k})\subset C^{0}([0,T],Y)
satisfies the conditions from the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem. Clearly
(f_{k})\subset C^{0}([0,T],Y)
is uniformly bounded. Let
s,t\in[0,T]
. We estimate

\|f_{k}(t)-f_{k}(s)\|_{Y}\leq C\|f_{k}(t)-f_{k}(s)\|_{X}\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f_{k}^{\prime}(t)\|_{X}|t-s|\leq C\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\|f_{k}\|_{C^{1}([0,T],X)}|t-s|.

Consequently, the uniform continuity of
f_{k}
follows. Finally, let
t\in[0,T]
. Since
f_{k}(t)
is bounded in
X
and
X\hookrightarrow\hookrightarrow Y
compactly, there exists a subsequence
k_{l}
such that
f_{k_{l}}(t)
converges in
Y
.

We now argue by induction. If the corollary is established for some
m
and
(f_{k})\subset C^{m+2}([0,T],X)
is bounded then
(f_{k})\subset C^{m}([0,T],X)
is bounded and
(f_{k}^{\prime})\subset C^{m}([0,T],X)
is also bounded. By the inductive hypothesis, we can pass to a subsequence that we again denote by
k
such that
f_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{f}
in
C^{m}([0,T],Y)
and
f_{k}^{\prime}\rightarrow\tilde{g}
in
C^{m}([0,T],Y)
. We now prove that
\tilde{f}^{\prime}=\tilde{g}
. Let
\varphi\in Y^{\prime}
with
\|\varphi\|_{Y^{\prime}}=1
. Since
m\geq 0
we have
\varphi\circ f_{k}\in C^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R})
with
(\varphi\circ f_{k})^{\prime}=\varphi\circ f_{k}^{\prime}
and
(\varphi\circ f_{k})^{\prime\prime}=\varphi\circ f_{k}^{\prime\prime}
. Given
t\in(0,T)
and small
h\in\mathbb{R}
we compute

\displaystyle\left|\varphi\left(\frac{f_{k}(t+h)-f_{k}(t)}{h}-f_{k}^{\prime}(t)\right)\right|=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{sh}\varphi(f_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t+r))drds\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{Y^{\prime}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t)\|_{Y}\left|\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{sh}drds\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t)\|_{Y}|h|.

(4.3.1)

Note that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t)\|_{Y}\leq\sup_{k}\|f_{k}\|_{C^{2}([0,T],Y)}=:\Lambda<\infty
. So, Estimate (
4.3.1
) is uniform over all
k
. Taking the supremum over all
\varphi\in Y^{\prime}
with
\|\varphi\|_{Y^{\prime}}=1
and subsequently letting
k\rightarrow\infty
shows

\left\|\frac{\tilde{f}(t+h)-\tilde{f}(t)}{h}-\tilde{g}(t)\right\|_{Y}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\frac{f_{k}(t+h)-f_{k}(t)}{h}-f_{k}^{\prime}(t)\right\|_{Y}\leq\Lambda|h|.

This shows that as a map from
(0,T)
into
Y
,
\tilde{f}
is differentiable with
\tilde{f}^{\prime}=\tilde{g}
. Since
\tilde{g}\in C^{m}([0,T],Y)
we deduce that
\tilde{f}\in C^{m+1}([0,T],Y)
.
Finally, using that

f_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{f}
in
C^{m}([0,T],Y)
and
f_{k}^{\prime}\rightarrow\tilde{g}=\tilde{f}^{\prime}
in
C^{m}([0,T],Y)
we deduce that
f_{k}\rightarrow\tilde{f}
in
C^{m+1}([0,T],Y)
.
∎

Theorem 4.3.3
.

There exists
N(n)
with the following property: For all
f\in C^{\infty}(M\times[0,T])
satisfying

\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu}\Delta_{g}^{m}f=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for all $0\leq m\leq N$}

there exists a function
u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\dot{u}+\Delta_{g}^{2}u=f&\displaystyle\textrm{in $M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}=0&\displaystyle\textrm{along $\partial M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle u(\cdot,0)=0&\displaystyle\textrm{on $M$}.\end{array}\right.

(4.3.2)

Proof.

By Sobolev embedding, we can fix
N(n)
such that
W^{2N,2}(M)\hookrightarrow\hookrightarrow C^{0,\gamma}(M)
compactly. Using Corollary
4.3.2
we get
C^{2}([0,T],W^{2N,2})\hookrightarrow\hookrightarrow C^{1}([0,T],C^{0,\gamma}(M))
compactly and by the definition of the space
C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
we have the continuous inclusion
C^{1}([0,T],C^{0,\gamma}(M))\hookrightarrow C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
. Hence

C^{2}([0,T],W^{2N,2}(M))\hookrightarrow\hookrightarrow C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{compactly}.

(4.3.3)

Let
\lambda_{k}
and
\phi_{k}
denote the Neumann eigenvalues and eigenfunctions from Theorem
2.3.5
. For
k\geq 0
, we define
c_{k}(t):=\langle\phi_{k},f(t)\rangle_{L^{2}_{g}(M)}
and for
\mu\in\mathbb{N}
we define
f_{\mu}:=\sum_{j=0}^{\mu}c_{j}(t)\phi_{j}
as well as

u_{\mu}(p,t):=\sum_{j=0}^{\mu}e^{-\lambda_{j}^{2}t}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\lambda_{j}^{2}s}c_{j}(s)ds\phi_{j}(p).

A direct computation shows that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\dot{u}_{\mu}+\Delta_{g}^{2}u_{\mu}=f_{\mu}&\displaystyle\textrm{in $M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial u_{\mu}}{\partial\nu}=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u_{\mu}}{\partial\nu}=0&\displaystyle\textrm{along $\partial M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle u_{\mu}(\cdot,0)=0&\displaystyle\textrm{on $M$}.\end{array}\right.

(4.3.4)

We note that
B_{1}u:=\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu}
and
B_{2}u:=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}
are the canonical boundary conditions associated to
\Delta_{g}^{2}
. We may therefore apply the Schauder estimate from Corollary
4.2.9
to the functions
u_{\mu}-u_{\nu}
and get

\|u_{\mu}-u_{\nu}\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}\leq C\|f_{\mu}-f_{\nu}\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}.

We prove that up to a subsequence, which we again denote by
\mu
,
f_{\mu}\rightarrow f
in
C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
. Once this is shown, we deduce that
u_{\mu}
is Cauchy in
C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
and hence convergent in
C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
. Putting
u:=\lim_{\mu\rightarrow\infty}u_{\mu}
and passing to the limit in Equation (
4.3.4
), we verify that
u
is indeed a solution of Equation (
4.3.2
).

First note that for each fixed
t\in[0,T]
we may use Theorem
2.3.5
to deduce that
f_{\mu}(\cdot,t)\rightarrow f(\cdot,t)
in
L^{2}(M)
. So, if along some subsequence
f_{\mu}
is convergent in
C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
, the limit has to be
f
as well. Using (
4.3.3
), it is sufficient to show that
f_{\mu}
is bounded in
C^{2}([0,T],W^{2N,2}(M))
to deduce that
f_{\mu}
is convergent along a subsequence in
C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
. For
l=0,1,2
and
k\leq N
we may use the assumption on
f
and integration by parts to compute

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}^{2k}\partial_{t}^{l}c_{j}(t)=

\displaystyle\langle(-\Delta_{g})^{k}\phi_{j},\partial_{t}^{l}f(t)\rangle_{L^{2}_{g}(M)}=\langle\phi_{j},(-\Delta_{g})^{k}\partial_{t}^{l}f(t)\rangle_{L^{2}_{g}(M)}.

Consequently, we learn that for all
k\leq N
and
l=0,1,2
we have the estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\lambda_{j}^{2k}\partial_{t}^{l}c_{j}(t)|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{l=0}^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\langle\phi_{j},(-\Delta_{g})^{k}\partial_{t}^{l}f(t)\rangle_{L^{2}_{g}(M)}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{l=0}^{2}\|\Delta_{g}^{k}\partial_{t}^{l}f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{g}(M)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(M,k)\|f\|_{C^{2}([0,T],W^{2N,2}(M))}^{2}.

(4.3.5)

For
0\leq k\leq N
and
l=0,1,2
we can use Estimate (
4.3.5
) and the fact that
\phi_{j}
is an
L^{2}
-orthonormal system to estimate

\|\partial_{t}^{l}\Delta_{g}^{k}f_{\mu}(t)\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}=\|\sum_{j=0}^{\mu}(-\lambda_{j})^{k}\partial_{t}^{l}c_{l}(t)\phi_{j}\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}=\sum_{j=0}^{\mu}\lambda_{j}^{2k}(\partial_{t}^{l}c_{j}(t))^{2}\leq C(M,N)\|f\|_{C^{2}([0,T],W^{2N,2}(M))}^{2}.

Since
\phi_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
f\in C^{\infty}(M\times[0,T])
we get
f_{\mu}\in C^{\infty}(M\times[0,T])
. Additionally, we can use that
\partial_{\nu}\Delta^{k}\phi_{j}=0
for all
j,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
to deduce that
\partial_{\nu}\Delta^{k}\partial_{t}^{l}f_{\mu}=0
for all
k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mu\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, we can use Lemma
2.3.3
to estimate

\|\partial_{t}^{l}f_{\mu}(t)\|_{W^{2N,2}(M)}\leq C(M,N)\sum_{k=0}^{N}\|\partial_{t}^{l}\Delta_{g}^{k}f_{\mu}(t)\|_{L^{2}(M)}\leq C(M,N)\|f\|_{C^{2}([0,T],W^{2N,2}(M))}^{2}.

Therefore
f_{\mu}
is bounded in
C^{2}([0,T],W^{2N,2}(M))
and the theorem follows.
∎

4.3.2
Initial and Boundary Values

Next, we wish to extend the existence result from Theorem
4.3.3
to allow for general
f\in C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
as well as initial and boundary values. To do this, we require some preparational results. The following theorem can, for example, be found in
[
14
]
(see Chapter 9, Theorem 9.25).

Theorem 4.3.4

(Collar Neighbourhood Theorem)
.

There exists a neighbourhood
U\subset M
of
\partial M
and a smooth diffeomorphism
F:\partial M\times[0,1)\rightarrow U
with
\Psi(p)=(p,0)
for
p\in\partial M
.

Lemma 4.3.5
.

For each
\mu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there exists a smooth function
\varphi_{\mu}\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that

\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu}\Delta_{g}^{\mu}\varphi_{\mu}=1\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\nabla^{k}\varphi_{\mu}|_{\partial M}\equiv 0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for all $k\leq 2\mu$}.

Proof.

Let
U\subset M
be a chart with
U\cap\partial M\neq\emptyset
and
\phi:U\rightarrow V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)
be the chart map. On
V
we define

f_{\mu}:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\ f_{\mu}(x):=\frac{-1}{(g^{nn})^{\mu+\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{x_{n}^{2\mu+1}}{(2\mu+1)!}.

Using the formulas

\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu}=-\frac{g^{ni}}{\sqrt{g^{nn}}}\partial_{i}
and
\Delta_{g}=g^{ij}\partial_{ij}-g^{ij}\Gamma^{k}_{\ ij}\partial_{k}
, it is straight forward to check that
f_{\mu}\circ\phi\in C^{\infty}(U,\mathbb{R})
has the desired properties on
U\cap\partial M
.

Let
U_{1},...,U_{m}
be charts that intersect and cover
\partial M
,
\eta_{i}
be a subordinate partition of unity and put
U:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}U_{i}
. For
1\leq j\leq m
we construct
\varphi^{(j)}_{\mu}:=f^{(j)}_{\mu}\circ\phi_{j}
as above and define

\varphi_{\mu}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\eta_{j}\varphi^{(j)}_{\mu}\in C^{\infty}(U,\mathbb{R}).

Finally, let
U^{\prime}\subset\subset U
be a neighbourhood of
\partial M
and
\zeta\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that
\operatorname{supp}(\zeta)\subset U
and
\zeta\equiv 1
on
U^{\prime}
. Then, by extending with
0
on
M\backslash U
, the function
\zeta\varphi_{\mu}\in C^{\infty}(M)
has the desired properties.
∎

Lemma 4.3.6

(Existence of an Extension Operator)
.

There exists a linear operator
E:C^{\infty}(\partial M)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(M)
such that
E[f]\big|_{\partial M}=f
for any
f\in C^{\infty}(\partial M)
.

Proof.

Using the Collar Neighbourhood Theorem
4.3.4
, there exists a neighbourhood
U
of
\partial M
and a diffeomorphism
F:U\rightarrow\partial M\times[0,1)
with
F(p)=(p,0)
for all
p\in\partial M
. We write
F(p)=(y(p),z(p))
. Given
f\in C^{\infty}(\partial M)
we first define

\tilde{f}\in C^{\infty}(U,\mathbb{R}),\ \tilde{f}(x):=f(y(x)).

Now, let
U^{\prime}\subset\subset U
be a neighbourhood of
\partial M
and
\zeta\in C^{\infty}(M,[0,1])
with
\zeta\equiv 1
on
U^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{supp}(\zeta)\subset U
. We define

E[f]:=\zeta\tilde{f}
by extending with
0
outside of
U
.
∎

We now to extend the existence result from Theorem
4.3.3
to general
f\in C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
.

Corollary 4.3.7
.

For every
f\in C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
there exists a function
u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\dot{u}+\Delta_{g}^{2}u=f&\displaystyle\textrm{in $M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}=0&\displaystyle\textrm{along $\partial M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle u(\cdot,0)=0&\displaystyle\textrm{on $M$}.\end{array}\right.

(4.3.6)

Proof.

First we consider the case where
f\in C^{\infty}(M\times[0,T])
. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be as in Theorem
4.3.3
. For
0\leq j\leq N
, we use Lemma
4.3.5
and choose smooth functions
\varphi_{j}
that satisfy

\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu}\Delta^{j}\varphi_{j}=1\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\nabla^{k}\varphi_{j}\big|_{\partial M}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for all $0\leq k\leq 2j$.}

Using the extension operator
E
from Lemma
4.3.6
, we define a function
T_{N}\in C^{\infty}(M)
recursively by

T_{0}:=\varphi_{0}E\left[\frac{\partial f}{\partial\nu}\right]\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636ptT_{k+1}:=T_{k}+\varphi_{k+1}E\left(\frac{\partial\Delta^{k+1}f}{\partial\nu}-\frac{\partial\Delta^{k+1}T_{k}}{\partial\nu}\right).

By construction,
T_{N}
is smooth and satisfies

\frac{\partial\Delta^{j}T_{N}}{\partial\nu}=\frac{\partial\Delta^{j}f}{\partial\nu}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for all $0\leq j\leq N$}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636ptT_{N}|_{\partial M}=0.

Let
\Omega
be a collar neighbourhood of
\partial M
, i.e. a neighbourhood such that there exists a diffeomorphism
\Psi=(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}):\Omega\rightarrow\partial M\times[0,1)
with
\Psi(p)=(p,0)
for
p\in\partial M
. For small
\epsilon>0
we choose a cutoff function
\zeta_{\epsilon}\in C^{\infty}([0,1),[0,1])
with the properties

\zeta_{\epsilon}\equiv 1\textrm{ on $[0,\epsilon]$},\hskip 14.22636pt\zeta_{\epsilon}\equiv 0\textrm{ on $[2\epsilon,1]$}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\|\zeta_{\epsilon}^{\prime}\|_{C^{1}([0,1])}\leq\frac{C}{\epsilon}.

On
\Omega
we define
\eta_{\epsilon}(p):=\zeta_{\epsilon}(\Psi_{2}(p))
. Extending
\eta_{\epsilon}
by zero to all of
M
, we obtain a smooth function, again denoted by
\eta_{\epsilon}
, on
M
with support in a small neighborhood
\Omega_{\epsilon}
of the boundary. We put

F_{\epsilon}:=f-\eta_{\epsilon}T_{N}

and claim that

F_{\epsilon}\rightarrow f
in
C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
. Showing this is, however, quite involved and we postpone it to the end of the proof. Since
\eta_{\epsilon}\equiv 1
on a neighbourhood of
\partial M
, we have

\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta}\Delta^{j}F_{\epsilon}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for all $0\leq j\leq N$}.

Using Theorem
4.3.3
, there exist functions
u_{\epsilon}\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
such that
u_{\epsilon}
solves problem (
4.3.6
) with
f
replaced by
F_{\epsilon}
.
Since
F_{\epsilon}\rightarrow f
in
C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
, we can use the Schauder estimate from Corollary
4.2.9
to deduce that
(u_{\epsilon})
is Cauchy in
C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
. We then obtain a solution to problem (
4.3.6
) as the limit
u:=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}u_{\epsilon}
.

For general
f\in C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
, we choose a sequence of smooth functions
f_{k}
such that
f_{k}\rightarrow f
in
C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
. By the first part of the proof, we get a sequence
(u_{k})\subset C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
such that
u_{k}
solves Problem
4.3.6
with
f
replaced by
f_{k}
. Using the Schauder estimate from Corollary
4.2.9
, we deduce that
(u_{k})\subset C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
is Cauchy and hence convergent. The limit
u:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}u
is the sought-after solution.

To see that
F_{\epsilon}\rightarrow f
in
C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
it suffices to show that
T_{N}\eta_{\epsilon}\rightarrow 0
in
C^{0,0,\gamma}(M)
. Let
\mathcal{A}=\set{(\phi_{\nu},U_{\nu})\ }{\ 1\leq\nu\leq m}
be a good atlas on
M
and
V_{\nu}:=\phi_{\nu}(U_{\nu})
. Denoting the
x
-argument by
\cdot_{1}
and the time argument by
\cdot_{2}
, we need to prove that for all
1\leq\nu\leq m

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\|T_{N}(\phi_{\nu}^{-1}(\cdot_{1}),\cdot_{2})\eta_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\nu}^{-1}(\cdot_{1}))\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}(V_{\nu}\times[0,T])}\rightarrow 0.

(4.3.7)

If
U_{\nu}
is an interior chart, establishing Equation (
4.3.7
) is trivial as
U_{\nu}\cap\operatorname{supp}(\eta_{\epsilon})=\emptyset
for all small enough values of
\epsilon
. Next, we consider a boundary chart. For the sake of simplicity in the representation, we drop the index
\nu
and consider a chart
\phi:U\rightarrow V
where
U\cap\partial M\neq\emptyset
and
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)
is open with
\partial_{S}V:=V\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times 0)\neq\emptyset
. We write
J(x,t):=T_{N}((\phi^{-1})(x),t)
and note that
J
is a smooth function with bounded derivatives that satisfies
J(x,t)=0
when
x_{n}=0
. Additionally, we write
\xi_{\epsilon}(x):=\eta_{\epsilon}(\phi^{-1}(x))
.

We start by establishing a technical estimate. Note that if
p\in\partial M\cap U
and
v\in T_{p}M
but
v\not\in T_{p}\partial M
, then
D\Psi_{2}(p)v\neq 0
as otherwise
D\Psi(p)T_{p}M\subset T_{p}\partial M\times 0
, which cannot be as
\Psi
is a diffeomorphism. Next, note that for any
x=(\vec{x},0)
we have
\frac{\partial\phi^{-1}(\vec{x},z)}{\partial z}\big|_{z=0}\not\in T_{\phi^{-1}(\vec{x},0)}\partial M
. Since
\Psi_{2}(\phi^{-1}(\vec{x},z))\geq 0
and
\Psi_{2}(\phi^{-1}(\vec{x},0))=0
, we obtain

\frac{d}{dz}\bigg|_{z=0}\Psi_{2}(\phi^{-1}(\vec{x},z))=D\Psi_{2}(\phi^{-1}(\vec{x},0))\frac{\partial\phi^{-1}(x,z)}{\partial z}\bigg|_{z=0}>0.

As
\phi:U\rightarrow V
is a good chart, we deduce that there exist
\theta>0
and
z_{0}>0
such that whenever
(\vec{x},z)\in V
and
0\leq z\leq z_{0}
we have

\frac{d}{dz}\Psi_{2}(\phi^{-1}(\vec{x},z))=D\Psi_{2}(\phi^{-1}(\vec{x},z))\frac{\partial\phi^{-1}(x,z)}{\partial z}\geq\theta>0.

This implies
\Psi_{2}(\phi^{-1}(\vec{x},z))\geq\theta z
whenever
z\in[0,z_{0}]
. There exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that for
\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
we have
\phi(U\cap\operatorname{supp}(\eta_{\epsilon}))\subset V\cap[0\leq z\leq z_{0}]
. As a consequence, note that when
2\theta^{-1}\epsilon<z<z_{0}
we obtain
\Psi_{2}(\phi^{-1}(\vec{x},z))>2\epsilon
and hence
\xi_{\epsilon}(\vec{x},z)
=0. By construction, if
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
z\geq z_{0}
we have
\xi_{\epsilon}(\vec{x},z)=0
. Thus there exists a constant
c_{0}>0
such that if
\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
we have
\xi_{\epsilon}((\vec{x},z))=0
whenever
z\geq c_{0}\epsilon
. From now on we always assume
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
.

Let
x=(\vec{x},x_{n})\in V
. If
x_{n}\geq c_{0}\epsilon
we have
\xi_{\epsilon}(x)J(x,t)=0
. When
0\leq x_{n}<c_{0}\epsilon
we may use
|\xi_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq 1
and
J((\vec{x},0),t)=0
to estimate

|J(x,t)\xi_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq|J(x,t)|\leq\|\nabla J\|_{C^{0}(V\times[0,T])}x_{n}\leq C\epsilon.

Since the constant in the above estimate is universal we have shown that
J\xi_{\epsilon}\rightarrow 0
in
C^{0}(V\times[0,T])
.

Now let
t_{1}\neq t_{2}\in[0,T]
and
x\in V
. If
x_{n}\geq c_{0}\epsilon
we have
|(J\xi_{\epsilon})(x,t_{2})-(J\xi_{\epsilon})(x,t_{1})|=0
. If
x_{n}\leq c_{0}\epsilon
we use
J((\vec{x},0),t)=0
to estimate

\displaystyle|\xi_{\epsilon}(x)J(x,t_{2})-\xi_{\epsilon}(x)J(x,t_{1})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|J(x,t_{2})-J(x,t_{1})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|t_{2}-t_{1}|\ \sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\partial_{t}J(x,t)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|t_{2}-t_{1}|\sup_{t\in[0,T],y\in V}|\nabla\partial_{t}J(y,t)\||x_{n}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\epsilon|t_{2}-t_{1}|.

Again, as the constant
C
in the above estimate is universal we obtain that
[\xi_{\epsilon}J]^{\operatorname{time}}_{\frac{\gamma}{4},V\times[0,T]}\rightarrow 0
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
.

To estimate the spatial Hölder seminorm, we first make the following observation. By definition
\xi_{\epsilon}:=\zeta_{\epsilon}\circ\Psi_{2}\circ\phi^{-1}
and hence
\|\nabla\xi_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}(V)}\leq\frac{C}{\epsilon}
. Let
x\neq y\in V
and
t\in[0,T]
. If
x_{n},y_{n}\geq C\epsilon
we have
|\xi_{\epsilon}(x)J(x,t)-\xi_{\epsilon}(y)J(y,t)|=0
. If
x_{n}\geq c_{0}\epsilon>y_{n}
we use
\xi_{\epsilon}(x)=0
and
J((\vec{y},0),t)=0
to estimate

\displaystyle|\xi_{\epsilon}(x)J(x,t)-\xi_{\epsilon}(y)J(y,t)|\leq

\displaystyle|\xi_{\epsilon}(x)-\xi_{\epsilon}(y)|\ |J(y,t)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{|\xi_{\epsilon}(x)-\xi_{\epsilon}(y)|}{|x-y|}\right)^{\gamma}|x-y|^{\gamma}(|\xi_{\epsilon}(x)|+|\xi_{\epsilon}(y)|)^{1-\gamma}\ \|\nabla J\|_{C^{0}(V\times[0,T])}|y_{n}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\nabla\xi_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}(V)}^{\gamma}\epsilon|x-y|^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\epsilon^{1-\gamma}|x-y|^{\gamma}.

Finally, if
x_{n},y_{n}\leq C\epsilon
we first use
|\xi_{\epsilon}|\leq 1
to estimate

|\xi_{\epsilon}(x)J(x,t)-\xi_{\epsilon}(y)J(y,t)|\leq|J(x,t)-J(y,t)|+|J(x,t)||\xi_{\epsilon}(x)-\xi_{\epsilon}(y)|.

The second term can be estimated just as in the previous case and is therefore bounded by
C\epsilon^{1-\gamma}|x-y|^{\gamma}
. Using
J((\vec{x},0),t)=J((\vec{y},0),t)=0
, we estimate

\displaystyle|J(x,t)-J(y,t)|=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{|J(x,t)-J(y,t)|}{|x-y|}\right)^{\gamma}(|J(x,t)|+|J(y,t)|)^{1-\gamma}|x-y|^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\nabla J\|_{C^{0}(V\times[0,T])}^{\gamma}\|\nabla J\|_{C^{0}(V\times[0,T])}^{1-\gamma}(|x_{n}|+|y_{n}|)^{1-\gamma}|x-y|^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\epsilon^{1-\gamma}|x-y|^{\gamma}.

So in total we have shown that
[\xi_{\epsilon}J]_{\gamma,V\times[0,T]}^{\operatorname{space}}\leq C\epsilon^{1-\gamma}
.

Combining the estimates from above, we have verified (
4.3.7
) also for boundary charts.
∎

We now extend the analysis to general boundary data.

Lemma 4.3.8
.

Let
f\in C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
,
h_{1}\in C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])
,
h_{2}\in C^{1,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])
and
u_{0}\in C^{4,\gamma}(M)
such that the following compatibility conditions are satisfied:

\frac{\partial u_{0}}{\partial\nu}=h_{1}(\cdot,0)\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u_{0}}{\partial\nu}=h_{2}(\cdot,0)

Then there exists a unique solution
u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\dot{u}+\Delta_{g}^{2}u=f&\displaystyle\textrm{in $M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}=h_{1},\ \frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}=h_{2}&\displaystyle\textrm{along $\partial M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle u(\cdot,0)=u_{0}&\displaystyle\textrm{on $M$}.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

Uniqueness follows immediately from the Schauder estimate in Corollary
4.2.9
. To establish existence, we first assume that
f,g,h
and
u_{0}
are smooth. For
k=0,1
, we use Lemma
4.3.5
to choose
\varphi_{k}\in C^{\infty}(M)
that satisfy

\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}^{k}\varphi_{k}}{\partial\nu}=1\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\nabla^{l}\varphi_{k}=0\hskip 5.69046pt\textrm{for all $0\leq l\leq 2k$}.

Using the extension operator
E
from Lemma
4.3.6
, we define

T_{0}:=\varphi_{0}E[h_{1}]\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636ptw:=T_{0}+\varphi_{1}E\left[h_{2}-\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}T_{0}}{\partial\nu}\right].

By construction,
w
is smooth and satisfies

\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu}w=h_{1}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu}\Delta_{g}w=h_{2}.

We define
w_{0}:=w(\cdot,0)
. Using Theorem
4.3.3
, we deduce that there exists a function
v\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\dot{v}+\Delta_{g}^{2}v=f-\dot{w}-\Delta_{g}^{2}w-\Delta_{g}^{2}(u_{0}-w_{0})&\displaystyle\textrm{in $M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial v}{\partial\eta}=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}v}{\partial\eta}=0&\displaystyle\textrm{along $\partial M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle v(\cdot,0)=0&\displaystyle\textrm{on $M$}.\end{array}\right.

We define
u:=v+w+(u_{0}-w_{0})
. Clearly
u(\cdot,0)=u_{0}
and

\dot{u}+\Delta_{g}^{2}u=f-\dot{w}-\Delta_{g}^{2}w-\Delta_{g}^{2}(u_{0}-w_{0})+\dot{w}+\Delta_{g}^{2}w+\Delta_{g}^{2}(u_{0}-w_{0})=f.

Finally, we use the compatibility conditions to check that

\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}=0+h_{1}+\frac{\partial u_{0}}{\partial\nu}-h_{1}(\cdot,0)=h_{1}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}=0+h_{2}+\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u_{0}}{\partial\nu}-h_{2}(\cdot,0)=h_{2}.

So, for smooth data, the theorem is proven. Now for the general case. Let
f_{\epsilon}
,
h_{1,\epsilon}
,
h_{2,\epsilon}
and
u_{0,\epsilon}
be smooth approximations of
f,h_{1},h_{2}
and
u_{0}
satisfying

\displaystyle\|f-f_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}<\epsilon,\hskip 5.69046pt\|h_{1}-h_{1,\epsilon}\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}<\epsilon,\hskip 5.69046pt\|h_{2}-h_{2,\epsilon}\|_{C^{1,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}<\epsilon,

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\textrm{and}\hskip 5.69046pt\|u_{0}-u_{0,\epsilon}\|_{C^{4,\gamma}(M)}<\epsilon.

In general, these approximations will not satisfy the compatibility conditions, so we need to modify them a little.
We define

\hat{h}_{1,\epsilon}:=h_{1,\epsilon}+\left(\frac{\partial u_{0,\epsilon}}{\partial\nu}-h_{1,\epsilon}(\cdot,0)\right)\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\hat{h}_{2,\epsilon}:=h_{2,\epsilon}+\left(\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u_{0,\epsilon}}{\partial\nu}-h_{2,\epsilon}(\cdot,0)\right).

Clearly
\hat{h}_{1,\epsilon}(\cdot,0)=\frac{\partial u_{0,\epsilon}}{\partial\nu}
and
\hat{h}_{2,\epsilon}(\cdot,0)=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u_{0,\epsilon}}{\partial\nu}
. However, we need to verify that
\hat{h}_{1,\epsilon}\rightarrow h_{1}
in
C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])
and
\hat{h}_{2,\epsilon}\rightarrow h_{2}
in
C^{1,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])
. To do so we use the compatibility condition
\partial_{\nu}u_{0}=h_{1}(\cdot,0)
to estimate

\displaystyle\|\hat{h}_{1,\epsilon}-h_{1,\epsilon}\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{\partial u_{0,\epsilon}}{\partial\nu}-h_{1,\epsilon}(\cdot,0)\right\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{\partial u_{0,\epsilon}}{\partial\nu}-\frac{\partial u_{0}}{\partial\nu}\right\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])}+\|h_{1}(\cdot,0)-h_{1,\epsilon}(\cdot,0)\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])}

\displaystyle\leq\|u_{0,\epsilon}-u_{0}\|_{C^{4,\gamma}(M)}+\|h_{1}-h_{1,\epsilon}\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])}

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon.

Proving
\hat{h}_{2,\epsilon}\rightarrow h_{2}
in
C^{1,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])
is achieved by repeating essentially the same argument. Since
u_{0,\epsilon}
,
\hat{h}_{1,\epsilon}
and
\hat{h}_{2,\epsilon}
are smooth functions that satisfy the compatibility conditions, the first part of the proof implies the existence of a unique solution
u_{\epsilon}
to the problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\dot{u}_{\epsilon}+\Delta_{g}^{2}u_{\epsilon}=f_{\epsilon}&\displaystyle\textrm{in $M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial u_{\epsilon}}{\partial\nu}=\hat{h}_{1,\epsilon},\ \frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u_{\epsilon}}{\partial\nu}=\hat{h}_{2,\epsilon}&\displaystyle\textrm{along $\partial M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}(\cdot,0)=u_{0,\epsilon}&\displaystyle\textrm{on $M$}.\end{array}\right.

(4.3.8)

Using the Schauder estimate from Corollary
4.2.9
it follows that
u_{\epsilon}
is Cauchy in
C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
and thus converges to a function
u
in
C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
. Passing to the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
in Equation (
4.3.8
), we deduce that
u
is the sought after solution.

∎

4.3.3
The Method of Continuity

The following is a standard result and a proof can, for example, be found in Gilbarg’s and Trudinger’s
[
9
]
(see Chapter 5, Section 2, Theorem 5.2).

Theorem 4.3.9

(The Method of Continuity)
.

Let
B
be a Banach space,
V
be a normed vector space and let
L_{0},L_{1}
be bounded linear operators from
B
into
V
. For each
t\in(0,1)
put

L_{t}:=(1-t)L_{0}+tL_{1}

and suppose that there is a constant
C
such that

\|x\|_{B}\leq C\|L_{t}x\|_{V}

for all
x\in B
and
t\in[0,1]
.
Then,
L_{0}
is surjective if and only if
L_{1}
is surjective.

Combining Lemma
4.3.8
and Theorem
4.3.9
, we obtain the following existence result.

Lemma 4.3.10
.

Let
L
be a 2-elliptic operator with smooth coefficients on
M
and denote by
B
the canonical boundary conditions
2
2

2

These automatically also have smooth coefficients.
. For every
f\in C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
,
h_{1}\in C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])
and
h_{2}\in C^{1,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])
satisfying
h_{1}(\cdot,0)=0
and
h_{2}(\cdot,0)=0
, there exists a unique solution
u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\dot{u}+Lu=f&\displaystyle\textrm{in $M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle B_{1}u=h_{1},\ B_{2}u=h_{2}&\displaystyle\textrm{along $\partial M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle u(\cdot,0)=0&\displaystyle\textrm{on $M$}.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.2.2
, the operator
L
induces a smooth metric
g_{L}
on
M
. We have
L_{1}:=L=\Delta_{g_{L}}^{2}+Hu
where
H
is a third-order, linear differential operator with smooth coefficients. Let
L_{0}:=\Delta_{g_{L}}^{2}
and, for
s\in(0,1)
, put

L_{s}:=(1-s)L_{0}+sL_{1}=\Delta_{g_{L}}^{2}+sH.

(4.3.9)

We denote by
\mathcal{B}_{s}
the canonical boundary conditions induced by
L_{s}
. Note that

\mathcal{B}_{s}u=\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu},\frac{\partial\Delta_{g_{L}}u}{\partial\nu}+s\langle J,\nabla^{2}u\otimes\nu^{\flat}\rangle\right)=(1-s)\mathcal{B}_{0}u+s\mathcal{B}_{1}u.

(4.3.10)

Here
J
is the
(3,0)
-tensor field from Lemma
4.2.2
. For
k=1,3
we put
C^{k,0,\gamma}_{0}(\partial M\times[0,T]):=\set{v\in C^{k,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])\ }{\ v(\cdot,0)=0}
and define the spaces

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle:=\set{u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])\ }{\ u(\cdot,0)=0},\vskip-5.69046pt

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle:=C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])\times C^{3,0,\gamma}_{0}(\partial M\times[0,T])\times C^{1,0,\gamma}_{0}(\partial M\times[0,T]).

We equip
X
with the
\|\cdot\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}
norm and
Y
with the product norm of
\|\cdot\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])}
,
\|\cdot\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])}
and
\|\cdot\|_{C^{1,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])}
. In this way,
X
and
Y
become Banach spaces. Clearly
T_{0}:=L_{0}\times\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
T_{1}:=L_{1}\times\mathcal{B}_{1}
are linear and continuous maps from
X
into
Y
. For
s\in(0,1)
we put
T_{s}:=(1-s)T_{0}+sT_{1}
. Translating the Schauder estimate from Corollary
4.2.9
into this new notation, we get

\|u\|_{X}\leq C(s)\|T_{s}u\|_{Y}.

(4.3.11)

In view of Equations (
4.3.9
) and (
4.3.10
), the constant
C(s)
is actually independent of
s
. Indeed, the coefficients of the operator
T_{s}
are bounded independent of
s
and the metric
g_{s}
induced by
L_{s}
is constant, i.e.
g_{s}=g_{L}
for all
s\in[0,1]
. So, by the method of continuity, the lemma follows once we have shown that
T_{0}
is surjective. This amounts to showing that for
f\in C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
,
h_{1}\in C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])
and
h_{2}\in C^{1,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])
satisfying
h_{1}(\cdot,0)=h_{2}(\cdot,0)=0
, we can solve the problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\dot{u}+\Delta_{g_{L}}^{2}u=f&\displaystyle\textrm{in $M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}=h_{1},\ \frac{\partial\Delta_{g_{L}}u}{\partial\nu}=h_{2}&\displaystyle\textrm{along $\partial M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle u(\cdot,0)=0&\displaystyle\textrm{on $M$}.\end{array}\right.

This is true by Lemma
4.3.8
as the necessary compatibility conditions are satisfied.
∎

Using Lemma
4.3.10
, we now generalize to nonzero initial values.

Theorem 4.3.11
.

Let
M
be a smooth, compact connected manifold with boundary
\partial M\neq\emptyset
and
T>0
. Let
L
be a 2-elliptic operator with smooth coefficients and denote by
B
the canonical boundary conditions. Let
f\in C^{0,0,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
,
h_{1}\in C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])
,
h_{2}\in C^{1,0,\gamma}(\partial M\times[0,T])
and
u_{0}\in C^{4,\gamma}(M)
such that the compatibility conditions

B_{1}u_{0}=h_{1}(\cdot,0)\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636ptB_{2}u_{0}=h_{2}(\cdot,0)

are satisfied. Then, there exists a unique solution
u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
to the problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\dot{u}+Lu=f&\displaystyle\textrm{in $M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle B_{1}u=h_{1},\ B_{2}u=h_{2}&\displaystyle\textrm{along $\partial M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle u(\cdot,0)=u_{0}&\displaystyle\textrm{on $M$}.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

We put
\tilde{f}:=f-Lu_{0}
,
\tilde{h}_{1}:=h_{1}-B_{1}u_{0}
and
\tilde{h}_{2}:=h_{2}-B_{2}u_{0}
. Then,
\tilde{h}_{1}(\cdot,0)=0
and
\tilde{h}_{2}(\cdot,0)=0
. So, by Lemma
4.3.10
, there exists a solution
v\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,T])
to the problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\dot{v}+Lv=\tilde{f}&\displaystyle\textrm{in $M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle B_{1}v=\tilde{h}_{1},\ B_{2}v=\tilde{h}_{2}&\displaystyle\textrm{along $\partial M\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle v(\cdot,0)=0&\displaystyle\textrm{on $M$}.\end{array}\right.

We get a solution to the original problem by defining
u:=v+u_{0}
. Finally, the uniqueness follows from the Schauder estimate in Corollary
4.2.9
.
∎

4.4
An Elliptic Problem

Using the parabolic Schauder estimates, we now derive elliptic Schauder estimates. Throughout this section, we assume that
g
is a smooth Riemannian metric on
M
.

Lemma 4.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a good atlas on
M
,
L
be a 2-elliptic operator of class
C^{0,\gamma}(M)
on
M
and let
B=(B_{1},B_{2})
define
L
-compatible boundary conditions of class
C^{3,\gamma}(\partial M)\times C^{1,\gamma}(\partial M)
. Then for any
u\in C^{4,\gamma}(M)

\|u\|_{C^{4,\gamma}(M)}\leq C\left(\|Lu\|_{C^{0,\gamma}(M)}+\|B_{1}u\|_{C^{3,\gamma}(\partial M)}+\|B_{2}u\|_{C^{1,\gamma}(\partial M)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}\right)

(4.4.1)

Here
C
depends only on
M
,
\gamma
,
\Lambda:=\|L\|_{C^{0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}+\|B_{1}\|_{C^{3,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M)}+\|B_{2}\|_{C^{1,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M)}
and
\theta:=\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}(L)
.

Proof.

We define
U\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(M\times[0,1])
by letting
U(p,t):=tu(p)
. Note
U(\cdot,0)=0
and
U(\cdot,1)=u
. So, using the Schauder estimate from Theorem
4.1.6
, we find

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{4,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}\leq\|U\|_{C^{4,1,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M\times[0,1])}\leq C\big(

\displaystyle\|\dot{U}+LU\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M\times[0,1])}+\|B_{1}U\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,1])}

(4.4.2)

\displaystyle+\|B_{2}U\|_{C^{1,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,1])}+\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|U(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}\big).

Since
U(p,t)=tu(p)
, we have the estimates

\displaystyle\|B_{1}U\|_{C^{3,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,1])}\leq C\|B_{1}u\|_{C^{3,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M)},

(4.4.3)

\displaystyle\|B_{2}U\|_{C^{1,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M\times[0,1])}\leq C\|B_{2}u\|_{C^{1,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M)},

(4.4.4)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|U(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)},

(4.4.5)

\displaystyle\|\dot{U}+LU\|_{C^{0,0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M\times[0,1])}\leq C(\|u\|_{C^{0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}+\|Lu\|_{C^{0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}).

(4.4.6)

Inserting Estimates (
4.4.3
), (
4.4.4
), (
4.4.5
) and (
4.4.6
) into Estimate (
4.4.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{4,\gamma}(M)}\leq C\big(

\displaystyle\|Lu\|_{C^{0,\gamma}(M)}+\|u\|_{C^{0,\gamma}(M)}+\|B_{1}u\|_{C^{3,\gamma}(\partial M)}

(4.4.7)

\displaystyle+\|B_{2}u\|_{C^{1,\gamma}(\partial M)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}\big).

By Ehrling’s lemma
\|u\|_{C^{0,\gamma}(M)}\leq\epsilon\|u\|_{C^{4,\gamma}(M)}+C(\epsilon)\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}
. Inserting into Estimate (
4.4.7
) and choosing
\epsilon
small enough, the lemma follows.
∎

Unlike in the parabolic case, it is not possible to remove the
L^{2}(M)
-norm on the right-hand side of Estimate (
4.4.1
). To see this, we consider the elliptic equation

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{g}^{2}u=0\hskip 52.63777pt\textrm{in $M$,}\\
&\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{along $\partial M$.}\end{aligned}\right.

(4.4.8)

This equation has the non-trivial solution
u=1
and hence we can not drop the term
\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}
in Lemma
4.4.1
. This is, however, the only issue. We formalize this statement in the following corollary.

Corollary 4.4.2
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
4.4.1
, there exists a constant
C=C(M,\gamma,\Lambda,\theta)
such that for every
u\in C^{4,\gamma}(M)

\|u\|_{C^{4,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}\leq C\left(\|\Delta_{g}^{2}u\|_{C^{0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}+\left\|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}\right\|_{C^{3,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M)}+\left\|\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}\right\|_{C^{1,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M)}+\left|\int_{M}ud\mu_{g}\right|\right).

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we will not make the dependence of
C
on
M
,
\gamma
,
\Lambda
and
\theta
explicit. Let
u\in C^{4,\gamma}(M)
,
f:=\Delta_{g}^{2}u
,
h_{1}:=\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}
and
h_{2}:=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}
. We compute

\int_{M}fud\mu_{g}=\int_{M}u\Delta_{g}^{2}ud\mu_{g}=\int_{M}(\Delta_{g}u)^{2}d\mu_{g}+\int_{\partial M}u\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}-\Delta_{g}u\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}dS_{g}.

Rearranging this identity, introducing a small parameter
\epsilon>0
and using Young’s inequality, we get

\int_{M}(\Delta_{g}u)^{2}d\mu_{g}\leq C(\epsilon)\left(\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}^{2}+\|h_{1}\|_{C^{3,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M)}^{2}+\|h_{2}\|_{C^{1,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial M)}^{2}\right)+\epsilon\|u\|_{C^{4,\gamma}_{\mathcal{A}}(M)}^{2}.

(4.4.9)

We put
\bar{u}:=\fint_{M}ud\mu_{g}
. Clearly
\int_{M}u-\bar{u}d\mu_{g}=0
. So, using Lemma
2.1.1
and Young’s inequality with a small parameter
\delta>0
, we may estimate

\displaystyle\int_{M}u^{2}d\mu_{g}=

\displaystyle\int_{M}u^{2}d\mu_{g}-|M|\bar{u}^{2}+|M|\bar{u}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}(u-\bar{u})^{2}d\mu_{g}+|M|\bar{u}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\int_{M}|\nabla u|^{2}d\mu_{g}+\bar{u}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C\left(\int_{\partial M}u\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}dS_{g}-\int_{M}u\Delta_{g}ud\mu_{g}+\bar{u}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(\delta)\left(\int_{\partial M}uh_{1}dS_{g}+\|\Delta_{g}u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}+\bar{u}^{2}\right)+\delta\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}.

Choosing
\delta>0
small enough, we may absorb
\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}
to the left-hand side. Inserting Estimate (
4.4.9
) and yet again using Young’s inequality with the already introduced small parameter
\epsilon
, we obtain

\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}\leq C(\epsilon)\left(\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}(M)}^{2}+\|h_{1}\|_{C^{3,\gamma}(\partial M)}^{2}+\|h_{2}\|_{C^{1,\gamma}(\partial M)}^{2}+\bar{u}^{2}\right)+\epsilon\|u\|_{C^{4,\gamma}(M)}^{2}.

Inserting this estimate into the estimates from Lemma
4.4.1
, we obtain

\|u\|_{C^{4,\gamma}(M)}\leq C(\epsilon)\left(\|f\|_{C^{0,\gamma}(M)}+\|h_{1}\|_{C^{3,\gamma}(\partial M)}+\|h_{2}\|_{C^{1,\gamma}(\partial M)}+|\bar{u}|\right)+\epsilon\|u\|_{C^{4,\gamma}(M)}.

Choosing
\epsilon>0
small enough, we may absorb
\epsilon\|u\|_{C^{4,\gamma}(M)}
to the left-hand side and deduce the corollary.
∎

Using a similar argument as in the proof of Theorem
4.3.3
, we now establish the following existence theorem.

Theorem 4.4.3
.

There exists
N(n)
with the following property: For all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
satisfying

\int_{M}fd\mu_{g}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu}\Delta_{g}^{m}f=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for all $0\leq m\leq N$}

there exists a function
u\in C^{4,\gamma}(M)
such that

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{g}^{2}u=f\hskip 52.63777pt\textrm{in $M$,}\\
&\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{along $\partial M$,}\\
&\int_{M}ud\mu_{g}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.4.10)

Proof.

By Sobolev embedding, we can fix
N(n)
such that
W^{2N,2}(M)\hookrightarrow\hookrightarrow C^{0,\gamma}(M)
compactly.
Let
\lambda_{k}
and
\phi_{k}
denote the Neumann eigenvalues and eigenfunctions from Theorem
2.3.5
. For
k\geq 0
, we define
c_{k}:=\langle\phi_{k},f\rangle_{L^{2}_{g}(M)}
and note that by assumption
c_{0}=0
. For
\mu\in\mathbb{N}
we define
f_{\mu}:=\sum_{j=1}^{\mu}c_{j}\phi_{j}
as well as

u_{\mu}:=\sum_{j=1}^{\mu}\frac{c_{j}}{\lambda_{j}^{2}}\phi_{j}.

From direct computation it follows that the functions
u_{\mu}
satisfy the problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{g}^{2}u_{\mu}=f_{\mu}\hskip 52.63777pt\textrm{in $M$,}\\
&\frac{\partial u_{\mu}}{\partial\nu}=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u_{\mu}}{\partial\nu}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{along $\partial M$,}\\
&\int_{M}u_{\mu}d\mu_{g}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.4.11)

Applying the Schauder estimate from Corollary
4.4.2
to the functions
u_{\mu}-u_{\nu}
shows

\|u_{\mu}-u_{\nu}\|_{C^{4,\gamma}(M)}\leq C\|f_{\mu}-f_{\nu}\|_{C^{0,\gamma}(M)}.

We prove that up to a subsequence, which we again denote by
\mu
,
f_{\mu}\rightarrow f
in
C^{0,\gamma}(M)
. Once this is shown, we deduce that
u_{\mu}
is Cauchy in
C^{4,\gamma}(M)
and thus convergent in
C^{4,\gamma}(M)
. Putting
u:=\lim_{\mu\rightarrow\infty}u_{\mu}
and passing to the limit in Equation (
4.4.11
), we verify that
u
is a solution of Equation (
4.4.10
).

First, note that we may use Theorem
2.3.5
to deduce that
f_{\mu}\rightarrow f
in
L^{2}(M)
. So, if along some subsequence
f_{\mu}
is convergent in
C^{0,\gamma}(M)
, the limit has to be
f
as well. To show that
f_{\mu}
is convergent in
C^{0,\gamma}(M)
, it is sufficient to show that
f_{\mu}
is bounded in
W^{2N,2}(M)
due to the choice of
N
. For
k\leq N
, we may use the assumption on
f
and integration by parts to compute

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}^{2k}c_{j}=

\displaystyle\langle(-\Delta_{g})^{k}\phi_{j},f\rangle_{L^{2}_{g}(M)}=\langle\phi_{j},(-\Delta_{g})^{k}f\rangle_{L^{2}_{g}(M)}.

Consequently, we learn that for all
k\leq N
, we have the estimate

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|\lambda_{j}^{2k}c_{j}|^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\langle\phi_{j},(-\Delta_{g})^{k}f\rangle_{L^{2}_{g}(M)}^{2}=\|\Delta_{g}^{k}f\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}\leq C(M,k)\|f\|_{W^{2N,2}(M)}^{2}.

(4.4.12)

Using Estimate (
4.4.12
), we deduce that for
0\leq k\leq N

\|\Delta_{g}^{k}f_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}=\|\sum_{j=0}^{\mu}(-\lambda_{j})^{2k}c_{j}\phi_{j}\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}=\sum_{j=0}^{\mu}\lambda_{j}^{2k}c_{j}^{2}\leq C(M,k)\|f\|_{W^{2N,2}(M)}^{2}.

Note
\phi_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
\partial_{\nu}\Delta_{g}^{k}\phi_{j}=0
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. So, for all
\mu\in\mathbb{N}
we have
f_{\mu}\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
\partial_{\nu}\Delta_{g}^{k}f_{\mu}=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Using Lemma
2.3.3
, we may estimate

\|f_{\mu}\|_{W^{2N,2}(M)}\leq C(N)\sum_{k=0}^{N}\|\Delta_{g}^{k}f_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(M)}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N}C(M,N,k)\|f\|_{W^{2N,2}(M)}^{2}\leq C(M,N)\|f\|_{W^{2N,2}(M)}^{2}.

Hence
f_{\mu}
is bounded in
W^{2N,2}(M)
and the theorem follows.
∎

We now have to essentially repeat the analysis we provided in Subsection
4.3.2
with minor tweaks to adapt to the elliptic case.

Corollary 4.4.4
.

Let
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(M)
such that
\int_{M}fd\mu_{g}=0
.
Then, there exists a function
u\in C^{4,\gamma}(M)
such that

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{g}^{2}u=f\hskip 52.63777pt\textrm{in $M$,}\\
&\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{along $\partial M$,}\\
&\int_{M}ud\mu_{g}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.4.13)

Proof.

We first consider the case where
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Following the proof of Corollary
4.3.7
we construct a function
T_{N}\in C^{\infty}(M)
that satisfies

\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}^{j}T_{N}}{\partial\nu}=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}^{j}f}{\partial\nu}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for all $0\leq j\leq N$}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636ptT_{N}\big|_{\partial M}=0.

We define
\eta_{\epsilon}
as in the proof of Corollary
4.3.7
. Recall that
\eta_{\epsilon}=1
on a small neighborhood of
\partial M
. We define

F_{\epsilon}:=f-\eta_{\epsilon}T_{N}+\fint_{M}\eta_{\epsilon}T_{N}d\mu_{g}.

Using the properties of
T_{N}
and
\eta_{\epsilon}
it follows that
F_{\epsilon}
satisfies

\int_{M}F_{\epsilon}d\mu_{g}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta}\Delta_{g}^{j}F_{\epsilon}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for all $0\leq j\leq N$}.

By Corollary
4.4.4
, there exist functions
u_{\epsilon}\in C^{4,\gamma}(M)
such that
u_{\epsilon}
solves Problem (
4.4.13
) with
f
replaced by
F_{\epsilon}
. Following the argument in the proof of Corollary
4.3.7
, we get
F_{\epsilon}\rightarrow f
in
C^{0,\gamma}(M)
. The Schauder estimate from Corollary
4.4.2
implies that
(u_{\epsilon})
is Cauchy in
C^{4,\gamma}(M)
and so we obtain a solution to Problem (
4.4.13
) as the limit
u:=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}u_{\epsilon}
. In general, we choose smooth functions
f_{k}\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that
f_{k}\rightarrow f
in
C^{0,\gamma}(M)
. By the first part of the proof, there exist solutions
u_{k}
to Problem (
4.4.13
) with
f
replaced by
f_{k}
. The Schauder estimate from Corollary
4.4.2
implies that
(u_{k})
is Cauchy in
C^{4,\gamma}(M)
and so we obtain a solution to Problem (
4.4.13
) as the limit
u:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}u_{k}
.
∎

We now extend the analysis to general boundary data.

Corollary 4.4.5
.

Let
f\in C^{0,\gamma}(M)
,
h_{1}\in C^{3,\gamma}(\partial M)
,
h_{2}\in C^{1,\gamma}(\partial M)
such that the following compatibility condition is satisfied:

\int_{M}fd\mu_{g}=\int_{\partial M}h_{2}dS_{g}

(4.4.14)

Then there exists a unique solution
u\in C^{4,\gamma}(M)
to

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{g}^{2}u=f\hskip 77.67589pt\textrm{in $M$,}\\
&\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}=h_{1},\ \frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}=h_{2}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{along $\partial M$,}\\
&\int_{M}ud\mu_{g}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.4.15)

Proof.

Uniqueness follows from the Schauder estimate from Corollary
4.4.2
. For existence, we follow the proof of Lemma
4.3.8
. First, we assume that
f
,
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are smooth. For
k=0,1
let
\varphi_{k}\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that

\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}^{k}\varphi_{k}}{\partial\nu}=1\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\nabla^{l}\varphi_{k}\big|_{\partial M}=0\hskip 5.69046pt\textrm{for all $0\leq l\leq 2k$}.

Using the extension operator
E
from Lemma
4.3.6
, we define

T_{0}:=\varphi_{0}E[h_{1}],\hskip 14.22636ptT_{1}:=T_{0}+\varphi_{1}E\left[h_{2}-\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}T_{0}}{\partial\nu}\right]\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636ptw:=T_{1}-\fint_{M}T_{1}d\mu_{g}.

By construction,
w
is smooth and satisfies

\int_{M}wd\mu_{g}=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\partial w}{\partial\nu}=h_{1}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}w}{\partial\nu}=h_{2}.

Using the compatibility condition (
4.4.14
), we compute

\int_{M}f-\Delta_{g}^{2}wd\mu_{g}=\int_{M}fd\mu_{g}-\int_{\partial M}\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}w}{\partial\nu}dS_{g}=0.

So, by Corollary
4.4.4
we know that there is a function
v\in C^{4,\gamma}(M)
satisfying

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{g}^{2}v=f-\Delta_{g}^{2}w\hskip 15.6491pt\textrm{in $M$,}\\
&\frac{\partial v}{\partial\nu}=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}v}{\partial\nu}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{along $\partial M$,}\\
&\int_{M}vd\mu_{g}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

We define
u:=v+w
and compute

\Delta_{g}^{2}u=f-\Delta_{g}^{2}w+\Delta_{g}^{2}w=f\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\int_{M}ud\mu_{g}=\int_{M}vd\mu_{g}+\int_{M}wd\mu_{g}=0.

Additionally, we have by construction

\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}=\frac{\partial v}{\partial\nu}+\frac{\partial w}{\partial\nu}=h_{1}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}u}{\partial\nu}=\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}v}{\partial\nu}+\frac{\partial\Delta_{g}w}{\partial\nu}=h_{2}.

So, for smooth data, the corollary is proven. Now for the general case. Let
f_{k}
,
h_{1,k}
and
h_{2,k}
be smooth such that
f_{k}\rightarrow f
in
C^{0,\gamma}(M)
,
h_{1,k}\rightarrow h_{1}
in
C^{3,\gamma}(\partial M)
and
h_{2,k}\rightarrow h_{2}
in
C^{1,\gamma}(\partial M)
.
In general, these approximations will not satisfy the compatibility condition, so we need to modify them a little. We define

\tilde{f}_{k}:=f_{k}-\frac{1}{|M|}\left(\int_{M}f_{k}d\mu_{g}-\int_{\partial M}h_{2,k}dS_{g}\right).

When
k\rightarrow\infty
, we have
f_{k}\rightarrow f
and
h_{2,k}\rightarrow h_{2}
. Since
f
and
h_{2}
satisfy the compatibility condition (
4.4.14
), we deduce that
\tilde{f}_{k}\rightarrow f
in
C^{0,\gamma}(M)
when
k\rightarrow\infty
. Additionally, we have

\int_{M}\tilde{f}_{k}d\mu_{g}=\int_{M}f_{k}d\mu_{g}-\left(\int_{M}f_{k}d\mu_{g}-\int_{\partial M}h_{2,k}dS_{g}\right)=\int_{\partial M}h_{2,k}dS_{g}.

So, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
we get a solution
u_{k}\in C^{4,\gamma}(M)
of Problem (
4.4.15
) with data
\tilde{f}_{k}
,
h_{1,k}
and
h_{2,k}
. Using the Schauder estimate form Corollary
4.4.2
we deduce that
u_{k}
is Cauchy in
C^{4,\gamma}(M)
and therefore converges to a solution of (
4.4.15
) with data
f
,
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
.
∎

4.5
A Decay Estimate

In general, an estimate of the form derived in Corollary
4.2.9
cannot be improved in the sense that the Schauder constant has a time dependence. Still, under additional assumptions, more can be proven. In this section we focus on
M=\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}:=\set{(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\ }{\ \omega_{3}\geq 0}
equipped with the metric
\delta
induced by the standard metric on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Points on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
,
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}
and
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{-}:=\set{(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\ }{\ \omega_{3}\leq 0}
are always denoted as
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3})
. We denote the Laplace-Beltrami operator by
\Delta
and the exterior normal along
\partial\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}
by
\nu
. Additionally, we abbreviate
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}):=L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+})
.

We require the following proposition. For a proof, we refer to
[
4
]
. (For the density statement, see Chapter 2, Section 8, Subsection 3, Theorem 2.34. For the statement concerning the eigenvalues, see Chapter 3, Section 3, Proposition 3.5. Finally, for the characterization of the spaces
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
, see Chapter 2, Section 1, Subsection 1, Example 2.6 combined with Chapter 2, Section 1, Subsection 3, Definition 2.7)

Proposition 4.5.1

(Laplace Eigenfunctions on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
)
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let

E_{k}:=\set{u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})\ }{\ -\Delta u=k(k+1)u\textrm{ on }\mathbb{S}^{2}}
. Then the following statements hold:

•

\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\bigcup_{k=0}^{\infty}E_{k}\right)
is dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
.

•

E_{0}=\mathbb{R}
and, putting
f_{i}:\mathbb{S}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\ f_{i}(\omega)=\omega_{i}
,
E_{1}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\set{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}}
.

Using Proposition
4.5.1
, we now investigate the spectrum of the Neumann Laplacian on
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}
.

Lemma 4.5.2
.

Using the notation from Theorem
2.3.5
: The eigenvalues of the Neumann Laplacian on
(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+},\delta)
have the following properties:

•

\lambda_{0}=0
and
\phi_{0}=\sqrt{2\pi}^{-1}
.

•

\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=2
and one can choose
\phi_{i}(\omega)=\sqrt{\frac{3}{2\pi}}\omega_{i}
for
i=1,2
.

•

\lambda_{k}\geq 6
for
k\geq 3
.

The proof we present is essentially taken from the proof of Lemma 4 in
[
3
]
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+})
satisfy the problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta\phi&=\lambda\phi\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{in $\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}$},\\
\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\nu}&=0\hskip 21.33955pt\textrm{along $\partial\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

We define the function

\bar{\phi}:\mathbb{S}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\ \bar{\phi}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\phi(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3})&\displaystyle\textrm{if $\omega_{3}\geq 0$},\\
\displaystyle\phi(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},-\omega_{3})&\displaystyle\textrm{if $\omega_{3}<0$}.\end{array}\right.

Since
\partial_{\nu}\phi=0
, it follows that
\bar{\phi}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+})\subset W^{1,2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. Additionally
\bar{\phi}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+})
,
\bar{\phi}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{-})
and
-\Delta\bar{\phi}=\lambda\bar{\phi}
separately on
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}
and
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{-}
. We claim that
\bar{\phi}
is a weak solution of
-\Delta\bar{\phi}=\lambda\bar{\phi}
. To see this, let
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
and denote the exterior unit normal along
\partial\mathbb{S}^{2}_{-}
by
\tilde{\nu}
. Using
\partial_{\nu}\bar{\phi}=0
along
\partial\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}
and
\partial_{\tilde{\nu}}\bar{\phi}=0
along
\partial\mathbb{S}^{2}_{-}
, we use integration by parts to deduce

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\langle\nabla\bar{\phi},\nabla\psi\rangle d\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}}\langle\nabla\bar{\phi},\nabla\psi\rangle d\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{-}}\langle\nabla\bar{\phi},\nabla\psi\rangle d\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}}\frac{\partial\bar{\phi}}{\partial\nu}\psi dS-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}}\Delta\bar{\phi}\psi d\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}+\int_{\partial\mathbb{S}^{2}_{-}}\frac{\partial\bar{\phi}}{\partial\tilde{\nu}}\psi dS-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{-}}\Delta\bar{\phi}\psi d\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\lambda\bar{\phi}\psi d\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}.

By Proposition
2.3.2

\bar{\phi}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. Using the notation and statements from Proposition
4.5.1
, there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
f\in E_{k}
such that
\langle f,\bar{\phi}\rangle\neq 0
. Consequently

\lambda\langle\bar{\phi},f\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}=-\langle\Delta\bar{\phi},f\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}=-\langle\bar{\phi},\Delta f\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}=k(k+1)\langle\bar{\phi},f\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}

and hence
\lambda=k(k+1)
. The lemma follows from Proposition
4.5.1
. Indeed, either
k\geq 2
and
\lambda\geq 6
or
k\in\set{0,1}
and consequently
\lambda\in\set{0,2}
. If
\lambda=0
then
\bar{\phi}\in E_{0}
and hence
\phi
is constant. If
k=1
, then
\bar{\phi}\in E_{1}
and there exist
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\phi(\omega)=\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}\omega_{i}
. Finally
a_{3}=0
, since
0\overset{!}{=}\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\nu}=a_{3}
.
∎

By Lemma
4.5.2
, the kernel of
\Delta(\Delta+2)
is the span of
\phi_{0}
,
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
(when imposing zero boundary values). For a function
\chi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+})
we define the components
\chi^{\parallel}

parallel
to and
\chi^{\perp}

orthogonal
to the kernel as

\chi^{\parallel}:=\frac{\langle\chi,1\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+})}}{\langle 1,1\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+})}}1+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{\langle\chi,\omega_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+})}}{\langle\omega_{i},\omega_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+})}}\omega_{i}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\chi^{\perp}:=\chi-\chi^{\parallel}.

Here, in a typical abuse of notation, we have used the symbol
\omega_{i}
also as the map
\omega\mapsto\omega_{i}
.

For
f\in C^{0,0,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}\times[0,T])
,
h_{1}\in C^{3,0,\gamma}(\partial\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}\times[0,T])
,
h_{2}\in C^{1,0,\gamma}(\partial\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}\times[0,T])
and
u_{0}\in C^{4,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+})
satisfying

\frac{\partial u_{0}}{\partial\nu}=h_{1}(\cdot,0)\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\partial\Delta u_{0}}{\partial\nu}=h_{2}(\cdot,0)

(4.5.1)

we consider the parabolic problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\dot{u}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta(\Delta+2)u=f&\displaystyle\textrm{in $\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu}=h_{1},\ \ \frac{\partial\Delta u}{\partial\nu}=h_{2}&\displaystyle\textrm{along $\partial\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}\times[0,T]$},\\
\displaystyle u(0)=u_{0}&\displaystyle\textrm{on $\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}$}.\end{array}\right.

(4.5.2)

Since
u_{0}
satisfies Equation (
4.5.1
), we may use Theorem
4.3.11
and deduce that Problem (
4.5.2
) has a unique solution
u\in C^{4,1,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{+}\times[0,T])
.

Theorem 4.5.3

(Decay Estimate)
.

Let
